In this is so, it carries the present struggle forwa rd into a very dangerous stage, and a stage which may be supplemented by reductions in wages to win ch the men would not submit,and which would end if further strikes and lockouts. Thus easily could the great labour war be forced on and the national in- dustry destroyed. If Mr. Richie can look at the signs of the times, he would not sit_mucb longer in silent seclusion and inactivity, with an unexhausted Con- ciliation Act in the pigeon-holes of the Board of Trade.

Further particulars are available of the official investigation made in India concerning the groat earthquake of the 12th of June last. It has trans- pired that the area which the shock was felt in- cluded the whole of the Irrawaddy valley and delta. An important report has been received from the Superintendent of Telegraphs, Assam, describing how, when communications trots Shillong were be- ing re-opened,it was found that so long as the earth was used awe return the operator was subject to electric shocks, some of considerable severity. This as well as the frequent interruptions of communica- tion which accompanid the shocks, ceased as soon as a second wire was used as a return.

The interest taken in the colonial cricket matches was never so great as on the present occasion. The amount of money spent in telegraphing the score and the comments runs into heavy figures every day. There are three or four di,stinct reports, and the City man who takes an intr3rest in the game cannot wait for his paper until be goes out for lunch. It was a big solicitor I heard of who kept a big client waiting ten minutes whilst be studied the details in his special evening paper. If the out- lay be great, I believe the London evenings and the provincial evenings find that it pays. They are able to have most of the business to themselves, as en- th usia-,tq want to be well posted the moment "close of play" come out on the contents bills."

As an evidence of the maintained en thusiesm of the Salvation Army,tbe meeting in the Albert Hall on Wednesday night is a concrete fact. Six thousand ardent sympathisers foregathered and five thousand applications for tickets had to be refused. The ob- ject was to bid farewell to General Booth on his tour through the United States and Canada. A let- ter was read from President At'Xinley conveying his opinion that the Army. had every right to be proud of its philanthropic work. The American Ambassador, Lord Loch, Lord Strathcona,the Agent General for South Australia. the Agent-General for New Zealand, and Sir Walter Bespnt sent letters regretting inability to attend, which were highly appreciated as showing the sympathy of statesmen and public men with the movement. But the "General" like Frederick the Great, is a believer in e numbers when the fighting force can be made to move as on man, and the way in which hie every word was taken up by the serried throng evidently gave him satisfaction. General Boothia sixty-eight, but his associates agree that he is the hardest worker in the Army, and his speech was free from any particle of vanity.

--- One of the brightest of the new year parties was given at the Guildhall on Wednesday evening. The occasion was the gathering together of some 1,300 children belonging to the Ragged School Union to a banquet and entertainment at the invitation of Alderman Treloar. From all quarters of 81umdom they came—from Whitechapel an(l Ifoxton, from Battersea and Limehouse and the Borough, till the spacious building was crowded to overflowing. Some were clean and some were dirty,but all were hungry, As a result, I,ooolb, uf roast beef and 170 plum puddings which the Alderman and his friends had provided rapidly disappeared. In addition, 4,000 hampers had been sent out to bed-ridden children. The band of the City police played popu- lar airs during dinner, and, busy as the juveniles were in'other direotiono,they never failed to 'loin in the chonts." Great was the cheering when the. tables bad been cleared and an army of waiters were discerned carrying in boxes of crackers, the gift of Messrs. Smith and Son, and deafening was the artillery that followed. Still more cheering heralded the approach of the Lord Mayer and Lady Mayoress and various officials of the Corporation. who made a tour of the hall and took up their places in the west gallery. A photograph of the gathering was taken by the flashlight,and a couple of short speeches followed. net boys of the Bellen- den-road Bosrd School performed a cantata, and there was a magic lantern display and other de- lights.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

"PAPA, what is intuition?" "Intuition; my child, is what your mother claims to have when she makes a good guess." JACK "Dick doesn't seem cheerful. I under- stood at one time that he'd be happy to die for that girl." "Yes; but he didn't." "I wisii I knew how to act when I meet a baby. I always feel like a fool." "All you have to do is to act the way you feel." "Then you don't intend asking Maria to be your bridesmaid?" "No ; Jack used to be engaged to her, and the dear boy is absent-minded." "Dos your wife worry about you when you travel ?" "Not at all. She knows I always buy accident insurance."

HARRY : " What girl was that you had in tow last evening?" Willie (indignantly) : " What you are pleased to call tow is usually spoken of by people of culture as blonde tresses," WILLIAS : "If I should kiss you, would you call mamma?" Agnes : "Oh, dear, no. There are some things that one is too selfish to divide with any- body."

TEACHER : "Now, Johnny, do you understand thoroughly why I'm going to whip you?" Johnny: "Yes, teacher. You're in a bid humour this morning, and you've got to cane some one before you'll feel satisfied." • A Lam- a few days ago gave a plate of cold meat and a glass of beer to a beggar at the door. The man (who was born in the Emerald Isle) said when going away (after a lot of Irish blessings), "An' sure, me leddie, if yer feet were as big as yer „heart, it wouldn't be my corrns I should like ye for to be threading upon." A GOOD PRESCRIPTION. -" Dlctor," said he, "I'm a victim of insomnia. I can't sleep if there's the least noise—such as a cat, on the back fence, for instance." "This powder will be effective," replied the physican, after compounding a pre- scription. "When do I take it, doctor ?" "You don't take it. You give it to the cat, in a little milk."

MRS. YEAST : "I wish I could think of some- thing to keep my husband at home at nights." Mrs. Crimsonbeak : "Get him a bicycle." Mrs. Yeast: "That would take him out more than ever." Mrs. Crimsonbeak : "Oh, no, it wouldn't! My husband got one the day before yesterday, and the doctor says he won't be out for a month." EARLY RISING INSANITY. - Blinks: "Say, professor, do you believe that early rising tends to insanity?" Professor Smart: "Yes ; I think there is considerable truth in it." Blinks : "But a man to be insane must have a delusion of some sort. Now, what particular delusion have you ever known an early riser to be afflicted with?" Professor Smart "The delusion that he liked early rising." A DIFFERENCE. —lre had been telling what a meritorious scheme he had, and as he finished he drew a piece of paper from his pocket. "I have here"— he began. "I'll not subscrible a cent," interrupted the business man. " I don't believe in your alleged charitable scheme, and I'll not give you a cent for it." "But I don't want any money from you," protested the caller. "Then what do you want?" "I just want you to sign a petition to some one else to help it along. Your influence, you know"— "Oh, well, I'll do that; but I won't give you any money for such a swindle." REBELLED AT LAST.—" I've worked for this man ten years," soliloquized the mule, "for my board and lodgings, and the board has been mighty cheap. I've furnished my own clothes, all except my shoes. I never have had a day off. In all these years I've never made any complaint about the way I'm treated, but this thing of driving me down the avenue with a load of garbage in broad daylight at my time of life is a little too much, and I'm going to kick !" Whereupon he kicked, and it rained garbage all over the neighbourhood for fully half a minute AN Englishman who acted as land steward on a Scotch estate was once treating one of the farm servants to a glass of whisky for doing some extra work. The servant (a Scotsman) put on an air of disappointment at the smallness of the glass. The Englishman, to divert the workman's attention from his critical study, jauntily asked—" Do you know how glasses are made, umph l" "No, I dinna ken," answered the Scotsman. "Well," said the steward, "they are blown." "Aweel," replied the servant, holding up the glass for closer inspection, "he mann surely ha'e been insist awful short o' braith that blew this ane."

A TERROR OF A COOK.--The young husband was somewhat surprised when his wife came into the office. She opened the conversation at once. "I want enough money to go out of town for a few days." she said, "and you will have your own meals in the city." "Why, what does this mean ?" "It means just this. I got a messenger boy to come to the house for Bridget to tell her that she was wanted at her aunt's, and as soon as she was 'round the corner, I shut up the house and locked it and ran away. When she comes back she won't .find anyone there. We don't owe her anything, so it's all right, and I wanted to discharge her; but you know I never would tell her to go, and I knew you wouldn't dare, and don't you think your little wife knows pretty well how to manage? Say yes, now, or I'll break down and cry here in the office."

INNOCENCE.—Jones was leaving his home one windy morning, when a big board, weighing fifty pounds or more, fell from a partially-built house near, and stunned him so severely that he was laid up for days in bed. Shortly after he was about again, he one day met the builder himself, and commenced to explain the danger of leaving loose planks about in windy weather. The builder called to a workman standing near, and repeated what Jones had just told him, remarking, in chiding tones: "Did no one tell you that Mr. Jones had been injured through your carelessness?" The British workman scratched his head thought- fully, and at last) owned: "I'd a'most forgot, guv'nor ; but now I come to think, my mate did tell me that Jones 'd come in for a windfall o' fifty pounds ouite unexpected !"

CAPITAL" CHAT.

Sir Wil'iam Lockhart's trip home is opportune,in view of the marked dissatisfaction of the War Office at the way in which the lira campaign has been conducted. Unquestionably, in the transport arrangements the organisation of the expedition has been lamentably bad, and there is too much reason to fear that the officers in command have net been the best that could have been chosen. The frequent rearguard casualties have not failed to attract the attention of military experts at home who do not conceal their opinion that they could and should have been prevented by more skilful dispositions. The explanations that will be sought from Sir William Lockhart will be nu-r.erous. Per- haps they will lead to a considerable strengthening of the staff at the seat of war—at any rate that is what is hoped for both at home and in India. The pity of it is that inadequate preparations and ineffi- cient leadership, following upon the initial adminis- trative blunder, should have led to the loss of so many gallant soldiers. For it is by common con- sent admitted that the rank and file was never I made up of " better stuff" than in this ill-fa ed I campaign.

The sudden outbreak of fresh difficulties in the Soudan is confirmed by the hurried speed with which English regiments are now being moved forward. A silence dsep as the impenetrable silence of the Sphinx prevails at the Foreign Office, and the people who have to pay all the cost of our foreign policy and enterprises know nothing of what is happening, save what they glean from en- ergetic newspaper correspondents. What is occur- ring now around and above Khartoum is a striking condemnation of the policy of leisurely conquest in the Soudan to which Mr. Chamberlain attempted to give so much plausibility a couple of years ago. This cheap and easy system of warfare has allowed Ruesia and France to establish their influence in Abyasiniadind to use that country as a tool to assist in preventing our capture of Khartoum. It has at present placed us face to face with a new triple al- liance in the Ethiopian provinces—namely,France, Abyssinia, and the Khelifa—and with the probabi- lity that the French flag will be hoisted at Khar- toum long before we have reached Meternmeh. And it is the same all round the horison. The Foreign Office has either been asleep or impotent. Lord Rowton's company are building two more model lociaing-hounes.on the same lines as thorn° al- ready in existence ; but to meet the necessities of London, the enterprise would require to be widely varied. It is not impossible that similar buildings. for the poorer classes of women, will also be soon erected. The project has been suggested to Lord Rowton,in whose schemes I infer that her Majesty is taking an interent.from the fast of his being in- cluded in her dinner pnrty at Osborne on New Year's Lay. He shrinks, however, from extending the scheme to wesnen, and this may be expected to be soon taken up by another set of philanthro- pists. There are openings also for similar homes for the various clesses of respectable young men who are employed in different capacities in London and an enterprise of this kind is already uncler con- sideration in some moneyed quarters. What is suc- cessfully done in New 'York can be successfully done here, and I think we are within measurable distance of a great nimber of poor men and women's boarding houses being erected amidst this vast and bewildering population.

-- The French expedition which is said to he within easy reach of Khartoum is the Marchand expedi- tion, which, notwithstanding all the rumours of the last few months, does nct appear to have been broken up at all, If M. Marchand has entered into an alliance with the Khalifa he practically enters upon a state of war with this country. If a seizure nf Khartoum by him should be recognised by the French Government, then an international question of the greatest gravity will arise—nothing less, in fact, than a state of war with France, The succes- sive letters which have reached this country from time to time from members of the expedition dis- close the intense hatred of Enghnd with which its leaders are animated, and their determination to keep us out of Khartoum; and if that key of Egypt is seized by them short would be the official 'life of any Foreigh Minister in France who would order them to abandon it. The moral is that our Foreign (Tice has only wakened up to the situation when it is too late, and that if this morning's telegrams are true the New Year begins with omens much blacker than any which threatened us in the year which has just closed.

There were rumours of startling developments among the advanced Labour party on Monday night, and they are repeated on Tuesday, in connPc- tion with the engineers' strike. These do not refer to renewed negotiation s with the moderate , section of employers, hut to the possibility of I three or four of the most powerful unions ordering a strike for the purpose of disoNanising their own and several other imp-rtmt branches of industry dependent upon them. This project would strike se deeply and widely as to paralyse in effect the general labour of the country. It is true that com- munications have already passed between the more ardent spirits of the labour world here, end their equally ardent colleagues in the unions referred to, but the unions have a considerable way to travel before they could be brought to adopt n policy of that kind. So long as avenues of fresh negotiations are open between the engineers and their employers I no such policy would be cluntenanced, and if the I unions were not well fortified with funds it would end in general disaster to the trade union cause.

There is one circumstance, however, which may accelerate the fulfilment of the rumours referred to, and that is the possibility of the Employers' Feder- ation receiving subsidies from employers in other trades not directly aifected 1 y the strike and lock- out. THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, JANUARY 7 1898. 1 BERYL'S HUSBAND,

MRS. HARRIET LEWIS, AUTHOR OF 61 Dark wsai." I" Lady Kildare," "Sundered & Her Double Life," &e.

CHAPTER XXXIX. A DAWNING OF LIGHT.

In the first moment of his entrance into Octa via's room, Conyers did not see the slender figure in black at the corner of the hearth. His eyes rested on Octavia, Nyho stood, evilly exultant, near the door.

"Well, what is it?" he demanded. "What are you grinning at? What has happened? The earl isn't—" _ _ _

_ "Hush !" said Octavia, with a start and a quick glance over her shoulder, " I've been making a purchase, dear, and I want your opinion. Come, see this lovely embroidery. Miss Star—" Beryl rose slowly up, white as death. Conyers turned his gaze upon her, and glared at her. They confronted each other for a brief space, in a dead silence. Conyer's face grew livid. He stood as one at bay. Octavia's jeering laugh rang through the room. Beryl dropped back into her chair. Conyers muttered an oath so savage that even Octavia shrank back from him, and he strode toward his wronged young wife, his visage terrifying in its blazing wrath, its savage, uncontrollable fury. " You here!" he said, in a hissing voice. "You have tracked me then? You have broken your oath? You have told your lying story to this lady. You—"

"'Stop !" said Beryl, r-ith a stern voice, her sweet face grown stern also. "Are you this lady's husband, Dane Conyers ? " "You know it. Have you not told her?" "Then that story was all true. You had a wife living?" Conyers looked from Beryl to Octavio and back

This lady," said he, indicating Octavia, "is my lawful wife. How dare you come here ? How dare you track me—" _ .

" fhave not tracked you. I came here to bring some work which this lady engaged me to do. I did not know that you lived here," said Beryl. "I have discovered you in your real character by acci- dent. And this lady is your wife also ? " She looked at Octavia pityingly. Her rival flushed back a look of hatred. "She is my lawful wife," said Conyers, driven to desperation, yet struggling to maintain a calm front. "Octavia, what has this girl been telling you ? " "Nothing. I suspected that you had known each other, and contrived to bring you together in order to verify my suspicions," said Octavia, coolly. " Who is this girl, Rollyn ? "I knew her in Switzerland," he answered. "I had a passing fancy for her, and we went through the form ot marriage, but I took care that the form should lack the legality. Why, she is baso- born and poor, and was being educated to become a governess. I amused myself with her for a month and then left her. She found her way to England and entrapped Desmond into a bona fide marriage, and then ran away from him. That s her history." "Alt !" said Octavia. "Does your side of the story differ from this Miss Star?" "Is his name really Conyers—Rollyn Dane Con- yers ?" asked Beryl, gravely. " Yes, that is his name." "He pretended that that name was assumed. It is the name he was married under. We were married at Geneva, Switzerland, by an authorised clergyman, in the English church, in the presence of witnesses, on the twenty-fourth day of last August, and our marriag is duly registered,:' said Beryl, solemnly. "Was your marriage prior to that, madam?"

CHAPTER XL. PACE TO FACE.

Octavia caught her breath sharply. Conyers met her gaze with one of sullen fury and defiance. "You invited the woman here, madam," he said, ironically, "and I wish you joy of her. You can hear her trumped up stories if you choose. I did marry her, but the marriage was not legal. Her name is not Beryl Star. Heaven only knows her parentage !" Beryl's form quivered as under a blow. Conyers addressed himself to her.

"I am Lord Hawkhurst's heir, and shall be next Earl of Hawkhurst. Do you think I would ally myself to a nameless creature like you? Does it look reasonable? Our marriage was not legaL Find your clergyman and witnesses, and see what they will prove. You have no more right to my name than to that of Star. Dare to attempt to hunt me down, and you will rue the day you were born. Any charge against me most recoil on your- self. According to your own story, you have two husbands living!" "I have not come here to expose you, and I have nothing more to say," said Beryl, quietly. "I am poor and friendless, but I would die in the Thames sooner than take my position as your wife. It may be all as you say. I may have been cheated; the marriage may not have been legal ; you may have been previously married to this lady. Whatever the truth, I shall never seek to know it, I have no friends to be wounded by my misfortunes, and I am content to leave my case in the hands of Him who knows all. But I can never sink so low as to lay any claim to you, Dane Conyers. You and this lady need have no fear of me l" _ _

---- • lier lip curled ins. acorn she could not conceal. She arose and took up her scattered drawings, the most cool and self-posaeased of the three.

- - - "I am sure I don't fear you," said Octavia, Insolently. "You did right not to call yourself by my husband's name, girl. You need not trouble yourself to complete those embroideries for me. Rollyn, dear, will you open the door?" Conyers sprang forward and did her bidding. More, he went out into the hall with Beryl, and appealed to her, in a whispering voice, to keep his miserable secret and spare him from exposure. " Our marriage was not legal," he said, eagerly. "I imposed a fraud upon you. Octavla is my actual wife, but I loved you better than I have ever oved her. Will you keep your oath, Bay', not to betray the fact of our marriage—yours and mine?" "I shall never break my word, Dane. I promised to keep the secret 80 long as you let rue alone, I believe it is possible that I may be your lawful wife, but I am only too glad to be released from the hate- ful bonds our marriage imposed upon me. Ido not know quite what is right, nor quite what is the truth, but I cannot be doing wrong in leaving you and her in peace. Rest assured, I shall not expose you !" She moved toward the stair. lie went back into Octavia's room, heavy-hearted ad solely troubled.

Oetavia met him with the countenance of a fury. "Is that creatures story true?" she demanded. "You have no timid, ignorant school-girl to deal with now, whom a word can deceive. I deniand the truth from your lips, if you can speak the truth. Was that creature known as your wife? And were you married to her, as Bhe said?" " That creature's story in 80 far is true." "Was—is she your lawful wife ? " "No. There were irregularities that made the marriage null and void. She was pure and good, and thought herself my wife. If she had been well- born and rich, I should have made the marriage legal." "Can I believe you?" "You are better aware of your own capabilities in that line than I am," he replied, scoffingly. " Will the girl make a scandal ?" "No • I have her word—her sworn oath—that she will never proclaim our marriage. She's honour itself, and would go to the stake rathei than break her word. You have nothin,g to fear from ter." "But have you nothing to fear from me? Sup- pose I expose you to Lord Ha.wkliurst."

"And cut yourself oat of a home? Come, come Octavia. Let my faults alone. You were an in- carnate fraud, pretending to be an heiress and all that, and you couldn't have expected to ally your- self to an angel. I have overlooked your faults; you must overlook mine. To expose me is to ruin yourself."

Meanwhile, Beryl had descended the stairs. As she gained the lower hall the door of the library opened, and Lord Hawkhurst, attired for a journey, a hook in hand, came out, meeting her face to face.

He stood still, as in a species of fascination. Something in the pale, spirited, lovely face touched his heart; or it might have been the sad, uncon- scioueseeching in her vivid brown eyes, as they uplifted to him. He noted that she had a small, noble head, crowned with short, red curls; that kat biututy wail of a striking and glorious type, and

,

as* she had the bearing of—a lad-y. He slipposed her to be a morning visitor of Octavia. He raised his hat to her almost unconsciously, and halted, with a respectful gesture to her to pass on. Beryl bowed in acknowledgment of the cour- tesy and walked on. He looked after her, his hat still in his hand, his soul in his eyes. As she neared the vestibule, her small fur boa fell from her neck. She caught it, adjusted it, and in doing so detached the necklace she always wore, with its pendant ornament—the pigeon's-blood ruby. Lord Hawkhurst saw it fall • but before he could speak, Beryl had passed out of the door and was gone.

The earl hurried forward and caught up the ornament. A single glance at it was sufficient to plunge him into a most profound agitation. He clutched it tightly in his hand and bounded out into the street.

Beryl was not in sight, but a cab was whirling down the street, and he sprang to the conclusion that she was in it.

"Too late!" he muttered. "What was he doing here? I must know."

He put the necklace in his pocket, and strode back into the house. He deposited his hat in the hall and hurried upstairs into Mrs. Conyers's bou- dour. Octavia's voice bade him enter. Octavia herself met him just within the door, her face bearing traces of recent passion. "Is it you, my Lord?" she ejaculated, in a real alarm. "Won't you come in? This is an unexpec. ted honour." She believed for the moment that Beryl had told her story to his lordship. A moment's reflection assured her that there had been no time for such communication, and her courage began to return to her.

"Excuse my abrubtness, my dear," said Lord Hawkhurst. "I have not come for a visit, only to inquire the name of the young lady who passed out just now. Who was she, Octavia ?" The sickening sensation returned to Octavia's heart.

"Young lady ? " she faltered. "I have seen no young lady this morning." - -

- "She did not see you then? Strange ! She came down the stairs. She was unmistakably a lady. I am sorry to have disturbed you. I will ask Butters. I ought, indeed, to have done so at first."

A consultation between his lordship and Butters would inevitably result in a discovery to the earl that the visitor had been up to Mrs. Conyers's apartments. Octavia took the alarm. "Stay," she said, pretending to reflect. "Was the person about whom you are inquiring, a young girl dressed in black 1" "Yes, yes." " Has she red hair?"

"Yes; she has red hair." "I remember, now," said Octavio.. "Why, she is but just gone. You called her a young lady,' and that misled me. She is only a sewing girl whom I have employed to work embroidery for me. She just came to fetch home her work. There it is on the table yonder. I should BO like your opinion upon it. I paid her a very extrava• ga.nt price for rt."

"A sewing-girl !" repeated the earl, mechanic- ally. " What is her name ?" "Mary Jones," said Octavia, uttering the first name that occurred to her. "She is supporting her invalid parents, she told me. A very worthy young woman, sir."

"Yes, yes, no doubt," said the earl, breathing heavily. "And her name is Mary Jones? I—l thought—excuse me, my dear; I will go down again. I shall not be in at dinner. lam about to make a little journey, and shall probably not be home till morning. Good-bye, my dear.' He held out his hand, shook hers warmly, pressed a kiss on her dark forehead, and hurried out.

"What do his inquiries mean ? " questioned oe- tavia of herself, when his heavy tread was heard descending the stairs. She hastened to her husband's dressing-room. Lord Hawkhurst returned to the lower hall and again put on his hat. He was anxious and ex- cited. He believed Octavia's story implicitly, yet some undefined impulse cause d him to question the hall-porter as he opened the door to go out. "Butters," said he, speaking with affected care- lessness, "the young lady who went out just now lost her necklace, as you saw. I must take means to restore it to her, if she should not return to inquire after it. Mrs. Conyers tells me that she is a sewing-girl. If she should come again let me know. I want to see her. Do you remember her name, Butters?"

"Yes, my Lord. She said it was Miss Star." " What I What name did you say" Star, my Lord."

Lord Hawkhurst passed down his steps and moved down the street. He signalled a cab at the nearest corner, and soon after was seated in a first. class compartment of a railway-coach, whirling swiftly through the winter day and the Surrey country, on his way to Starwood.

CHAPTER XLL LORD HAWKHURST AT STARWOOM

The short February afternoon was drawing to its close when Lord Hawkhurst drove in at the open gates of StArwood farm, and alighted at the hospitable porch.

The cabman blanketed his horse, and sank clown deeper into the warmth and protection of his man!. fold capes, while the earl rang the knocker loudly. Steps were heard shuffling over the bare floor of the hall, and old Margot, with a candle held far above her head. and with a peering gaze in her old sunken eyes, looked out at the visitor. "Is it news of the master ? " she asked, in a querulous voice. "Oh, it's a stranger, eh I Come in, sir._ Come in !"

'She flung the door open wide, and Lord Hawk- hurst entered the hall, which, uncarpeted and un- furnished, was dreariness itself. Old Margot, her candle still held above her head, the yellow light flickering with her movements led the way through the long hall to the kitchen, or " house- place " beyond. "There is no fire in the master's room," she said, apologetically. "Sit down, sir. Did you wish to see the master ? "

'I ho earl ;e-Plied in the affirmative, with a curious. glance about him and at the old housekeeper. "The master went away quite unoxpeetediy, last night," explained old Margot. "A gentleman came up from London with ill news, and the master went away with him, saying he should not return under a day or two. Perhaps, sir, you are come upon the same business as the other?"

_ She looked at the earl sharply. He rubbed his gloved hands together, but shook his head as he replied: "My business has no connection with that of any other. Can I not see Mrs. Star.

"The master is not married, si:." "Not married? Can I not see his sister—his father—"

"He has no sister, sir. And his father died, seventeen years ago, of a broken heart," said old Margot, as calmly as if she were mentioning a very common disease.

The earl had half risen. He sank back in his chair again. "A broken heart I That's a rare malady," he observed, but his voice had lost something of its calmness. "Is there no lady at the head of Mr. Star's house, my good woman? Has he no con- fidential friend, no trusted relative, with whom I can have speech for a few moments?" "There's no one but me, sir. The master lives here like a hermit, as one might say. I know all his family affairs better than any one beside him- self. I mind the master when he was a wee lad, as gay and lightsome as he is now hard and bitter. I I nursed Miss Jenny in those arms from the very hour of her birth. I know the Stars root and branch. If you want to talk of family affairs, you can talk to me almost as well as to the master him- self, sir."

There was a suppressed anxiety in the old woman's face and manner that declared her expec• taut and solicitous to hear some especial tidings. The earl hesitated. He was averse to speaking to this trusted old servant of her master's family affairs; but he, too, had heavy anxieties, and it seemed to him that he could not wait until he should be able to discover Mr. Star. He was not here to pry out secrets, but to ask a few questions which it would not be treason to her employer if old Margot should answer. While he deliberated, she exclaimed

"You need not be afraid to speak to me, sir. I know my place, and I should npt answer any ques- tions I ought not to. The master knows that he may trust me as himself. I may say that I brought him up, and Miss Jenny—" "Miss Jenny?" said the earl, huskily. "The master's sister, sir. She died young. She is buried near London. She was a great beauty, sir, and as innocent and pure_ RA an angetr-my poor

lainb F My poor lamb I" said old Margot:her voice suddenly changing to a sort of low, wailing tone.

The earl was silent for a brief space, and his face was averted. Presently he said, very quietly: "I am come hero, my good woman, to ask Mr. Star a very simple question. Perhaps you can answer it. Who is the girl known as Beryl Starr'

Old Margot uttered a quick ejaculation, and came nearer to the visitor, her features working, her dull old eyes emitting an unwonted gleam. "I was right ! " she exclaimed. "You have come upon the same business that took the master away. Where is Miss Beryl? Where is she ? " "Answer my questions first. First of all tell me who is the gentleman who came from London and took Mr. Star away with him?" "His name is Desmond, and he's married to Miss Beryl," said old Margot, flinging reticence to the winds under the belief that this stranger knew the entire story of Beryl's disappearance. "I heard him say that he had traced out the master, and believed Miss Beryl to belong to this family, and he took the master to London with him." "It was Noble Desmond. He is searching for his lost wife everywhere, poor fellow. I saw her to-day—" " To-day ? You saw her? Then she is not dead? She has not killed herself? She was safe Was she safe? Oh, sir, I am half mad with the trouble—"

"She was safe and well," said the earl, kindly. "She is earning her living with her needle." "Safe and well! The good Lord be praised!" sobbed the old woman, in an uncontrollable joy. "Oh, sir, I never slept last night for thinking of her. The poor disowned lamb! She's had a hard life, sir, from her very birth. She was brought up in a school in Switzerland, and never had a home. She never had any one to cuddle her up, as mothers do their children, to pet her, to love her. And when she came here a while ago, in the winter's cold, beggin' g as you might say, for a shelter and a kind word as you'd fling to a dog, the master pelted her with hard words as they were stones, and turned her out of the house, and she went out into the great world, sir' to earn her own bread, and barely seventeen, and a stranger in England! And when Mr. Desmond found the master out, and came here and said she was missing, the master turned white as a sheet. He thought as he had driven her to her death, as the world had proved too much for her, as she had worked and starved, and gone voluntarily to her death." The woman laughed and wept together. The earl was greatly moved by her agitation. Her words had smitten him as so many knife-thrusts. "You love Miss Beryl very dearly? " he said. "Yes, I love her, and I pity her. I love her because she has no one else to love her, because she has been misused all her life." " Was not Mr. Star kind to her?" " Kind ! He hated her. He would have been glad to have her die, although not by her own hand. That would have looked as if he had driven her to it."

"Why should he hate her! Had she ever harmed him ? "

"Never, except in her coming into the world." The earl started. "How (lid her birth wrong him?" he asked. " I cannot tell you, sir." "Is Beryl Star her real name?" "It is the name the master gave her, sir. She had no right to any." " Mrs. Beryl looked very pale and thin," he ob. served. " She has had a hard life, as you say. I do not wonder that Mr. Star feels burdened with remorse."

"Did you come to him with a message from her, sir ?"

"She did not send me, but I am come upon her account," replied the earl, evasively. "You need not be so reticent with me, my good woman. I presume any neighbours of yours could tell me all I want to kno

"No one in this neighbourhood knows of the existence even of Miss Beryl." "No one ? " The earl was surprised. "Is she not an acknowledged member of the family Z " . ..

pld Margot shoco'k her head. 1" What relation does she bear to Mr. Star?" "She is his niece."

An unguarded admission. Old Margot was angry with herself the moment she had made it. Its effect upon the earl was most marked and singular. He uttered a strange, low cry, and his form quivered as under a galvanic shock. He gasped for breath, and fell back in his chair. Old Margot ran to him in terror. As she approached, he feebly waved her back, and said hoarsely

am not well, I think. Don't disturb your. WI. I shall be myself presently. His niece I His niece, you said ' He removed his hat, and wiped his brows,vvhich were wet with sweat. Old Margot watched him closely, with new and strange ideas forming in her mind. There was a long silence. "This girl Beryl is the daughter of Jenny Star. Poor girl ! I must go hack to London by the first train. I must find her immediately. I can readily obtain her address. Oh I why did I not know! Why did I not guess?" His voice was broken; his soul agitated to its depths. " I don't remember having ever seen you before," said old Margot, "but I have my suspicions that you knew Miss Jenny. After she died there was a man came here to inquire after her. He seemed a lawyer sort of man. I think he had been sent here by Miss Jenny's lover, and the master thought so, too. The master told him that Miss Jenny was dead, and told him where she was buried. But the master did not tell him that Miss Jenny left a child? How could he? I think you have heard the whole story from Miss Beryl. I cannot think that you would make a mock of the good name of this family. The master has been hard and cruel, but no one knows what he has suffered. He loved Miss Jenny with a love that was real worship. She was his pride, his idol. He found her in a London lodg- ing-house alone. She gave birth to this child, Miss Beryl, and died. There was no wedding-ring upon I her finger; no husband beside her to cheer her last moments. She never even said that she was married. But I know that she was pure and good till the last. And—" Old Margot's eyes gleamed, and she raised her hand high above her head— "may God's bitterest curse light upon the husband who deserted her May his nights be sleepless, his days full of bitterness. May he be baulked in all his hopes and desires I" "All this aud more have come to him," said the earl, with a groan. "He who caused her death has been punished. She is avenged." He put on his hat and strode to the door, feeling blindly for the latch. Old Margot would have detained him further, but he hastened on to the outer door and gave himself egress. The cabman was asleep on the box. Lord Hawkhurst roused him up and entered the cab, and the vehicle rolled out of the Star wood grounds, and old Margot stood in the doorway gazing after it, a pale light behind her outlining her form. THE BEVERAGE OF THE PEOPLE.

Let us glance at the ordinary breakfast beverages of the people. Tea, even if properly infused, is only a stimulant. It is not a nourishing beverage, and as usually decocted is washy, trashy, and deleterious. Coffee, even when of the best, and prepared in p tion as you will find in the East, where Mahommedans are forbidden by their religion to use alcohol, is only a cardiac or heart stimulant. It increases for a short time the power of that organ without being in any sense of the word a nourishio.; beverage. Cocoa —The ordinary cocoa is not by any means a nourishing beverage. Now the Christmas and New Year festivities have come and gene,many of us are feeling just a little fiat. We had looked forward to them so long, and when at last the season cameet was so soon over. But there are otherienotably surly old cynics, who consider Christmas a nuisance, and think, with satisfaction, that almost 12 whole months must pass before they can again be bothered with all the nonseasicel rubbish,indulged in at this season of the year. Such members of the commu- nity however,are distinctly to be pitied. If we would be happy ourseives,we most make others happysaud this should be no difficult task,for the children and the poor are always with us. Christmas Day is almost the only one in the year when her Majesty allows herself a rest from letter writing. She has all her long life kept up a voluminous corres- pondence with members of her family. Her Private let- ters are rarely dictated, and the volumes of manuscript she has written would fill a very deep and substantial shelf. " Always your affectionate mother V.R.1.," is how the Queen signs herself in her letters to her sons and daughters. The quer Lion as to how the debts of the late'Duchess of Teck (which amount to over £30,000) are to be met, has yet to be decided. With regard to the statements that have been made from time to time concerning the disposal of the artistic treasures of White Lodge, as a means of raising this sum, such a mode of procedure would be impessible. When the Dachess of Teck occa- pied White Ledge in 1883, all she took with her was her plate, china, and jeweis, some objects of art, and otier personal property. All the furniture and almoseall the treasures of White Lodge, belong to the Queen. The late Duchesses dressers are now provided fer. One is to be allowed an annuity of £5), the other has the Queen for her mistress. Toe chief butler, too, is to be remembered when a vacancy occurs among Her Majesty's messengers. One of the duties of these func- I tionaries, by the way, is distinctly carious. When any members of the Royal Family are expected at a railway station, the messenger sent thither only waits to see them arrive, and then gallops rapidly back to let the I Queen know that her guests are actually on the way to her gates. It is to be regretted that poplin i 3 not making much progress. There are really only eight manufacturers of this excellent fabric in Irelacreend there seems te be no immediate demand for more. Tne feeling of to-day is rather for glistening silks that shine brightly and wear out quickly, and so create the necessity for something new. Poplin was first made in Avignon, In the fifteenth century, and was first introduced into Ireland, some say in the seventeentb. others the eighteenth century. A revival of plush is the latest item of note, in the millinery world.. The mode though is not likely to be long lived, so far as up-to-date womea are cencerned. Once " in," plush is usually taken up by" Arriet " with such enthusiastn, that it is very quickly " out" again. Just now however, it is in its first stage of hold, yet hesi- tating novelty, and therefore has its charm. The January sales are in full swing, and first and foremost among the bargains of the hour, coins furs. Fur capes are en evidence at less than half price, at many large establishments, and a purchase of this description could scarcely fail to prove an excellent investment, for U all specimens happened to be built full, and ample. last autumn, we shall be quite on the right elde this year, in the matter of material. Although tight-lacing is by no means so general as it wee, there CHI remain women, whose one ambition (judging by the size of the waists) would appear to be— an early grave. British women ridicule—and naturally too—the absurd custom which causes women in China to Cramp their feet, till walking becomes almost impos- sible, yet many of them do not give a thought, tipper- ently, to the absurdity and folly of compressing their lungs till they can scarcely breathe, their hearts till pal- pitation and faintine are produced, and other internal organs to such an extent that the displacement and dis- tortion must result in present suffering and future illness. The atheletic movement of the last few years has done much to counteract the evil of tight-lacing,but much re- mains yet to be hoped for, in this direction. The corset is of great antvity, but it was not used much in this country before the 11th and 12th centuries. In the 14th century a small waist for a women was an es tablished fact,and was regarded as essential to a bsauti-- fist figure. Dress was a great deal thought of in the reign I of Queen Elizabeth, and treat extremes were aspired to by every woman. Tne fashion of small waists canoe to each a pitch at the time of the French Revolutien, that a reaction set in, and for a time dress somewhat in the Greek style prevailed. We are all familiar with this I Style, and know it under the name of the "Empire." I ' might also say that women have not been alone in the folly of tight lacing, for men of fashion in the 16t cen- tury carried it to a great pitch, Henry the Third of France leading the m It appears that in many New York churches, a "church maid "is now employed ! Gowned in plain black, and adorned with a white cap and apron, she nioves swiftly yet silently aboitt,shows visitors to seats, lends hymn books,and imparts any desired information in connection with the church. This useful damsel also cares for the vestry,and gives the edifice many touches , of which the janitor is incapable. And there areet seems, across the Atlantic, over 200 women who have ben regularly ordained as ministers, beeides nearly three times as many evangelists and lay preachers. One can but fervently hope they will remain where they are. Women preachers have yet to prove themselves a success. So far as my own experience is Concerned, I have always found that there is more energy than argument about a feminine discourse. Paper bicycles have appeared upon the scene ! One owned by a bicycle agent, is now in use in Lendon,and a factory for the r roduction of bicycles of this descrip- tion is said to be contemplated. Bottles too, for use on board ship particularly, are being made of paper. In bad weather much damage has been done by the break- ing of bottles of wine and other liquors, in the store rooms of vessels, in spite of every precaution, bat the new paper bottles will, it is supposed, quite do away with this troable. Tney can be handled very roughly, without the least apprehension of damage or destruc- tion. If girls would retain well-shaped lips, they must re- frain from biting them. All the cold cream, lip Naivete and glycerine lotions introduced upon the toilet table will not reme ler the p ached, cracked appearance due to continual lip-biting. Girls frequently drift into this ugly practice through a foolian vanity that prompts them to " wear their dimples "all the time. Others bite their lips from nervousness' and sometimes if a girl has palelcolourless lips, she will try to revivify them by en occasional pressure with her teeth. WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A meeting of this. Council was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Alderman Wright) presided, and there were also present. Aldermen Sutton, Greenall, Hutchinson, Bolton. Councillors Roberts, Shaw Green, Plampton, Smethurst, Long- ton Bennett, Evans, Ellison, Burton, Tinuion, Peacocke, F. R. PROPOSED NEW POLICE STATION.—THE BATTLE OF THE SI rE3.--MAGIS CR ATE 3 AND CRIMINALS. The Watch Committee reported that having carefully considered the question of a site for the new police office it had resolved that the Council be recommended to select the sit) adjoining the Volunteer premises near the Town Hall. The MAYOR moved and Alderman Surrox seconded the adoption of the minutes. Councillor GRACE moved as an amendment that the minutes be confirmed with the exception of the resolu- tion relating to the new police offices. Ha felt that they had no right to cover up any open space in the centre of thetown,as the policy of every municipal body in modern times seemed to be to retain as many lungs as possible for the benefit of the people, and to build upon the proposed site would be a piece of folly which the ratepayers would not altogether approve of. The committee should take into cmiideration the question of the Drill Shed. 'roe Volunteers were the tenants of the Corporation, and the premises could be taken over and the police court and the bridewell built there, as well as anything else which might be required for the police administration of the town. The Volunteers could have the Old Barracks near Bulk Qaty railway station. The Corporation wou'd then have an oppor- tunity of widening Bath-street and making another approach to the Park and to the Town Hill. They could also, fix in Bath-street the bridewell as well as the drill accommodation, and the front or the Police Court could b- made to face the open space which it was the inten- tion of the committee to build upon, ant they could re- tain the laod as an opea space. It could be laid out in walks.

COUNCILLOR BENNE r r AND HIS PROPHESY. At a meeting of the Museum, Library,Arts,and Tech- nical Instruction Committee, it was resolved that he Corporation be requested to invest £l,OOO as " the Charles Middleton Bequest Fund." It was reported that the General Purposes Committee had decided not to proceed with the suggested Industrial Exhibition, that the committee wished to be informed of the probable cost of bolding an Exhibition of Art only, and it was resolved that the Art Gallery Sub-Committee be re- quested to inquire into the probable cost of holding an Art Exhibition.

Councillor ROBERTS, in moving the adeption of the minutes, said the committee had been considering the probable cost et the proposed Art Exhibition, and they were utterly unable to arrive at an estimate. In com- pany with the Town Clerk he visited Messrs. Agnew's, Manchester, last week, to ask them to give them some idea of the expenditure that would be involved, but they replied that until they could tell the number of pictures that would be accepted, where they had to come from, where they had to go back to,it was utterly impossible to give an estimate, as it was a question of labour and time. From the cost of other exhibitions he thought they would be able to ask the General Pur- poses Committee to guarantee a sum that would be com- paratively modest in amount. Councillor MONIES seconded the motion.

Councillor BENNETT congratulated himself on the ful- filment of his own prophecy. (Laughter.) Ile had said that in January they would either postpone the Indus- trial Exhibition for another six moaths or abandon it altogether. Nothing was more farcical than the pro- ceedings in connection with that exhibition, and he was glad that the committee had at last the heart to drop it altogether. They had been deluding the publics arid making themselves appear ridiculous. He hoped the same fate would not befall the Art Exhibition. There was some reason for holding it in the present year, as he was informed that this was the Jubilee year of the Museum. The exhibition would be a most interesting and instructive one, and it all depended upon the com- mittee putting their hearts into it and making it a SUCCESS.

Councillor H. ROBERTS said Councillor Bennett was one of the prominent members of the committee which he said had succeeded in deluding the public and making itself appear ridiculous. He was quite satisfied that he should apply the remarks to himself, but he (Councillor Roberts) had not on that or any other occasion at- tempted to delude the public,and he bad not any,further than he could help made himself appear ridiculous. He had been cautioned times innumerable about using strong language in regard to matters that he was bring- ing before the Council, and he had lived long enough to know that one did not further one's cause by that means. He, therefore, tendered the advice to another gentleman who was erring ha the same direction, that it I would not facilitate matters,or assist them to work more comfortably,if such language were used. The committee had abandoned the Industrial Exhibition because they realised more fully than Councillor Bennett had done what an enormous task they had before them. Things of that sort were not done by resolutions or elaborate and oratorical speeches. There was a -if hard work to be done. It was suggested in the earlier of the proposal that an expert should be em- ployed for the purpose of relieving them of a great deal of work. Be was very sorry to say that Councillor Bennett was the man whs put the block on that proposal. If the Council had not been in- duced to throw out that proposal the scheme would have been accomplished, and he thought successfully. It was not to be expected that members of the Council could give their personal attenticn and time to the development of an exhibition of that kind and do justice to their own business. That was the reason why some of them had sto3d on one side in regard to the exhibi- tion. The Art Exhibition would also involve a consi- derable amount', but nothing like the amount of work ' that an Industrial Exhibition would require. If they should be fortunate in getting other promises of assist- ance like that most valuable one which was made to them a few months ago by Mr. Holbrook Gasket!, their work would be comparatively easy. He did not despair of having an Art Exhibition that would be 1 satisfactory to the town generally. (Hear, hear.) Councillor BENNETT said he appreciated Councillor Robert's witticism, but when he endeavoured to "lump him with the committee in its ridiculous attitude he dis- claimed any responsibility whatever in regard to their 1 action. During the whole of the time the matter had been before the Council he had urged that it should be proceeded with without delay. This was the second time he had had to give a flat denial to the statement that he blocked the proposal to employ an expert. The minutes were then confirmed. THE ASYLUMS BOARD AND THE INTERMEN T OF BODIES.

In consequence of an application which had been re- ceived from the Lancashire Asylums Board, the commit- tee have passed the following resolution :—" That the Council be recommended to allow the bodies of persons dying at the Winwick Asylum to be buried at the Ceme- tery upon payment of double fees, ordinary fees to be charged for the interment of the bodies of persons from this borough dying in the asylum." The Borough Sur- veyor having reported that Howley Brook, behind the Cemetery, had become silted up, owing to the negligence of the owners of land below the Cemetery, it was re- solved that notice be served upsn the owners requiring them forthwith to clear out the brook% The minutes were e3nllrmed, on the motion of Coun- cillor Eveics, seconded by Councillor SHILCOCIC.

NOTES AND NEWS.

The manufacture of amateur prophets on a wholesale scale is the latest act of up-to-date American journalism For this purpose about a third of a page of a New York paper has been reserved for the writing of the prophecies which are to be sealed and sent to the editor, who will not have them opened until 1899, in order to verify the predictions of the prophets for the year just begun.

We so frequently have to to to America for pictur- esque—if not precise—news concerning ourselves that we read in the " New York Herald " without a spasm of surprise that Prince Arthur, the sin of the Duke of Con- naught, is the fag at Eton of the son of that austere Re- publican, Mr. W. W. Astor. By way of pointing the moral of this indignity, the "Herald" declares that " the fag in an English school is little more than a slave," and that the American boy "is practicllly lord and f: Queen Victoria's favourite grandson." How- ever, Queen Victuri:, may be somewhat reassured on learning that young Astor "is not :,-. nrd taskmaster, and the Prince has an easy time of it compared with other little boys who are obliged to do fag duty."

The Pope's daily life is nictured by a correspondent of the " Daily Trira;th." Lis is deicribed as now shortened in height by tlae painfully pronounced bead of the upper part of the body. Illness he never knows, in spite of the fragility of his frarhe and organism. Here is his day. Ile rises at tix is the morning after four

hours sleep. After coffee and milk, with a morsel of bread, he settles down to the work of the day. Through. out the morning be takes an occasional cup of broth,ani at one o'clock his mid-day meal, at which he drinks a glass of claret from a convent at Bordeaux. This sufficrs to secure him his daily siesta, which he take with his face to the light. Hs is very proud of his col. lection of birds, especially parrots, and has a vineyard to which he devotes much care.

The English residents in Shanghai have given a hearty welcome to the three cycling commissioners of" Travel," John Foster Fraser, Edward Lunn and Frank Lowe.whO have just accomplished the feat of cycling across China from Upper Burmah,the first time that such a thing he ever been carried througn. Since leaving London, in July 1898, the cyclists have traversed 14,322 milea.They all bear traces of the severe hardships they have under. gone and give a very bad account of the Celestial Em. vire as a field for cyclists. The roads were shocking and for many hundreds of miles they were obliged to carry their machines. By the people of the Western province they were hospitably treated on the whole, though ia Yung-Chang-fu they were mobbed by a erowd and had to take refuge in a temple. Armed with voluminous passports and red visiting cards several inches long, with their names in Cninese characters, they were from town to town interdiewiny: mandarins and missionaries, living.on pork and rice and becoming expert in the mat agement of chopsticks. During their journey an officer travelled on ahead. giving orders at all the military posts that their safety was to be looked after. Here and there they seem t have experienced the animosity of the Chine to the "foreign devil," as for instance at a TH. lage in the csntre of China. "Ss we spun into the town of Panchio,"write the travellers, "everybody was sitting out of dpnre, eating their evening rice. But rice bowls were pitched aside and chopsticks lost, in the scamper after us. We looked for some convenient inn. We asked the crowd to show us the way but they only grinned and yelled. One tall fellow, with only ens eye, gained tae plaudits of his friends by a little hustling. It was necessary to have a smack at that one-eyed Chinaman, if only to secure respect. Folks fell back. Then they set their dogs at us, fisrce pariah brutes that sniff a foreigner half a mile off. A. ferocious monster showed fight. In an instant one of us drew a revolver and the next moment the animal was lying dead with a bullet in its shoulder. Thereupon the crowd receded. We found a hotel and there was no more bother, When we rode away next morning everyone was respectful. But the only kow-tow we received was from the man whose dog WAS killed, though he took the precaution of lighting a taper at his door,either to propitiate us or as a safeguard against evil consequences." 'Generally speaking, how. ever,the worst they seem to have to contend against was the exuberant curiosity of the Celestiale, who were keen on exhibitions of cycling prowess,and had frequently to be put off by the excuses that the iron horses were tired and wanted a rest.

William Linton, wno died the other day in his Ameri. can home at Newhaven, was the famous wood-engraver to whom Walter Savage Lander, in a presentation copy of his " Imaginary Conversations of Greeks and Be. mans," referred as " a true patriot and a true poet." In his early days, when working for a wood engraver In London, he used ro go to Torn Hood's chambers in the Adelphi to fetch his queer pen and ink drawings for his "Comic Annual." He used to recall him 83 " a spare man (I fair stature, grave, but not ungenial." He knew Carlyle, but thoirght h'm a bad judge of men, and wag of opinion that Dickens missed his mark when he be- came a novelist. " His real vocation was as an actor of low comedy," he wrate " warm-hearted and sentimen- tal, but not unselfish, he was not the gentleman. There was no grace of manner, no soul of nobility in him." Li don succeeded Bewick as the leading representative of the slow obsolete a::: of wood engraving, on which he was the greatest living authority. He was a man of picturesque aspect, with leonine head and shaggy hair and beard, pyramidal forehead, and big, penetrating eyeg. Ruskin bought Brentwood, his locus° in the lake country.

THE EXAMINEE,

JANUARY 7ch, 1898.

SHOULD the whole of the present Looking Ahead. year be in harniony with its opening there is undoubtedly a very unsettled period in front of this country,and, indeed of the world generally. There is an un- mistakable disquietude in at least four out of the five Continents. Amongbt the five great tern toxin] divisions of this planet, Australasia alone appears to be in a sufficiently happy position to be able to boast that it is not making history—his- tery of a sensational type, that is, of course—at the present time. The antipodeans are not with- out their ambitions and their:unsettled questions, but just now their attention is mainly absorbed by the peaceful and friendly rivalries of the cricket field. It may perhaps be also said that the out- look so far as America is concerned is less threatening now than it was at the commence- ment of last year. The great trouble there has, of course, its origin in the condition of Cuba. That fertile but desolated island has on several occa- sions very nearly become a bone of contention be- tween the United States and Spain. The States have, however, displayed a forbearance which might have been thought impossible by these who have paid any attention to the " tall talk" of Yankee journalists, but which is, no doubt, largely due to the fact that those in authority know it is much easier to talk than to fight, especially when you have neither an army nor a navy in a very forward state of preparedness. The Spanish government, on its side, is beginning to show a more conciliatory disposition, so that taking one thing with another, there may not be, tl is year at all events, a "Cuban crisis" of a very serious character. In some other parts of the world the prospect is not quite so reassuring. Africa has been described by Lord SALISBURY as the plague of the Foreign Offices of Europe, and there is just a danger that it may become a worse plague than ever during the next few months The latest reports disclose the fact that the British Government is pushing forward with almost feverish energy the preparations for a further advance into the Soudan. What is the reason for this rather abrupt renewal of war- 117:activity on the Nile? It must be remem- bered that one of the cherished ambitions of British imperialists—and the present Government is imperialist if it is anything—is "to paint Africa red" from Egypt to Cape Colony—in other words to have an uninterrupted strip of British possessions from one end of the Dark Continent to the other. This scheme can only be carried into effect by effectively occupying the land, whether desert or fertile, lying between the most southern Egyptian outpost and the most northern portion of British Central Africa. But it is now known that the French have deter- mined to frustrate this big imperial scheme if 1 they can do so. A French expedition, or expedi- tions, starting from French ground in Africa, has been struggling for month's past to reach Khar- toum or the upper waters of the Nile before any British force could get there from Egypt. For anything that is definitely known this French ex- pedition may have already reached its destination and taken possession of some parts of the tipper Nile. If that be so what will Lord Saws/males next step be? Will ho quietly and humbly ac- quiesce in the destruction of all the plans that have been cherished for a British railway and telegraph from the north to the south of Africa P

he ith r a the an.

ter- if adi- has ler-

any For I ex- Ltion pper ny's BC- that and rica ?

. .

Or will he ask the French to evacuate territory which has been occupied with unfriendly intentions towards this country ? These questions will show that there are, or may he, some very knotty problems in statecraft to solve in Africa before the year is out. If we turn from Africa to Asia the prospect presented in China is not more cheerful or en- couraging. It is now generally understood that the activity which Russia and Germany are dis- playing in that part of the world is not calculated to be of any advantage to this country. English commerce with China is of considerably more value than that of all other countries put together, and the efforts of both Russia and Germany are made undoubtedly with the object of giving their own merchants exclusive advantages. There again, therefore, Lord SALISBURY is confronted with the duty of maintaining the rights and priveleges of the country he represents. There is an uneasy fear, even amongst Tories, that foreign govern- ments have come to the conclusion that Lord SALISBURY is not a very important factor in these international arrangements—that, in short, our Prime Minister if pressed sufficiently is sure to I give way in the end. That opinion is no doubt ased upo:a his weakness and vacillation in dealing with an Eastern Question a little nearer home. Lord SALISBURY'S extraordinary subservience to the Concert of Europe—that is to say to a com- bination of two or three despots—has won him no thanks and no friendships. It has only encour- aged these aggressive powers to promote other schemes whereby they can profit, largely at the expense of England. The result is that the new year opens upon a scene that is, to say the least, not auspicious, and that may very easily develepe serious complicatio,is.

A settlement of the dispute in the The Labour Struggle. engineering trade never seemed more hopeless than it .does at the present time. There may, of course, be a sudden change by the unexpected yielding of one ai le or the other, or by a break in the solid organ- isations which have faced each other during the last six months. But appearances do not eneour- age any expectation of that kind. The workmen baying, with practical unanimity, rejected the terms offered by the ernployers,the representatives of the employers declare that, on their side, there will be no amendment of those terms. In the meantime they are extending the lock-out. Both sides have, therefore, apparently settled down to a trial of endurance. It is generally assumed that, so far as funds are concerned, the resources of the roasters aro practically inexhaustible, though it is obvious that a stoppage of machinery and output must tell very heavily against some of the smaller and less we ilthy firms. With respect to the men, it is now more than ever a question of ways land means. The accumulated funds of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers have stood the drain upon them much better than was expected by many people at the commencement of the:dispute, but, even allowing for the larg levies paid by the engineers who are still at work, it is obvious that the locked-out men must now depend more than ever upon contribu- tions from the workmen of other trades. It is a very interesting and important question as far as the engineers are concerned, whether a regular and constant supply of funds from outside can be relied upon. The experience of the last six months does not supply the material for a very definite answer. Some, in fact many, trade unions have contributed steadily and handsomely for months past, but, on the other hand, there has been a strange lukewarmness or backwardness on the part of certain workmen's organisations which might have been expected to give a strong helping hat? (I. That there is strength in British trades u,nionism sufficient to back up the engineers in- "efinitely is undeniable, but whether there is the nece.ssary will or ability to apply that strength in all quarters is another matter.

Ihe scheme tor a jubilee industrial exhibition in Warrington-has had a chequered and lingering existence, but it appears to be now finally laid at rest. The General Purposes Committee has de- cided not to proceed with the exhibition, but it is inquiring into the probable cost of an art exhibi- tion, One does not feel too sanguine with regard to this art exhibition, but the committee certainly deserves credit for the extraordinary tenacity with which it has clung to the fragments of the jubilee programme. No doubt a mistake was made in the first instance in attempting to do too much, but on the whole the jubilee was by no means unworthily celebrated in Warrington. Even apart altogether

from any connection with the jubilee,an art exhibi- tion would be a very interesting event, consider jag the close relations with Warrington of a number of artists of more at less note, but whether or not it can be arranged on a sufficiently attrac- tive scale is a matter which the Art Gallery Sub- oraMittee will no doubt thoroughly consider.

The report of the Borough Surveyor which was presented to the Town Council on Tuesday bears out all that has been said as to the inade- quacy of the present bridge over the river, and the need for a new one. Mr. Longdin discredits any suggestion that the needs of the case might be fully met by any tinkering with the present bridge. The rate at which traffic is increasing seems to prove that any economical plan of that kind would soon ' turn out to he of the " penny wise and pound foolish" order. At the same time the £lO,OOO which it is estimated a new structure sixty feet wide would cost is a bit of a poser in the case of Warrington which has already so many big improvements on the list. No doubt it depends a good deal upon the County Council whether there is tO be a new bridge within a reason- I sonable time. It is said that the influential mem- bers of C.: e County Council who were in Warrington • last week were impressed with the need for an im- provement, and perhaps as ready a way as any Of convincing the county councillors would be to in- vite the whole of them down to see the state of traffic on a Wednesday when, according to the ob.ervations taken on December Bth, four vehicles and four or five times as many pedestrians pass over the bridge every minute. This in itself would show that Warrington does not want a new bridge liniply for the purpose of improving the navigation I nt the river.

It is common knowledge in the town that there was more drunkenness this year than ever before in Warrington during the Christ- mas holioltys. We do not know whether this iS to be ascribed to good trade or to the knowledge that extreme leniency would be shown by the police. It is, however, a noticeable fact that the number of persons dealt with for drunkenness this week has been exceedingly small, and to judge by the police records Warrington alight have been the Most sober town in the king- dom. Those who Raw the state of the streets on Christmas Eve, and again on Sunday and Boxing Day could not but notice the large number drunken persons. Even boys were to be seen walking cork-screw fashion,and the maudlin besot- ' •

===== 0002601_18980107_art0053.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====


===== 0002601_18980107_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
tedness of some people was disgusting to witness. It is to be hoped that when the Chief Constable presents his annual report ther will be no special credit taken for a decrease of drunkenness. There was enough drinking in Warrington during this Yuletide to last a twelvemonth.

The Fabian Society has issued an address " to trade unionists and all public-spirited citizens in the United Kingdom " with reference to the lock- out of engineers which contains one statement of very grave significance indeed. It is that " the Cabinet and Government officials are helping the federated employers, the Board of Trade by deli- berately refraining from using the powers given it by the Conciliation Act, and the Admiralty by con- niving at delay in the completion of our war ships.' If it be really true that the Admiralty, under the control of the Right lion. G. Joachim Goschen, is conniving at delay in the completion of British war ships, that public department is guilty of what can hardly be described as less than an act of stupend- ous treachery against this country. For what is the international situation ? The statesmen of all the leading countries in the world seem to have come to the conclusion that a great naval war might break out at any time in the immediate future, and they have therefore entered into a mad ace with each other in the construction of war- ships. So close is the rivalry that, it is said, even a delay of a few months in the construction of new vessels might lose this country the margin ,of superiority which is believed to be neces sary for its safety. Yet it is here actually charged against the Admiralty that it is conniving at this delay. This is a point that should be cleared up very quickly and very thor- oughly. The Fabian Society urges that mass meet- ings should be held and that parliamentary repre- sentatives should be made to speak out, and that political associations and trade societies should send up petitions insisting that the Government shall act on behalf of the nation and not of the federated employers. The Warrington Trades and Labour Council is, on the whole; a very modest or- ganisation, but this is a matter upon which it might fairly take a leading part. The member for Warrington is, on the platform, a great imperialist, and it would be interesting to know what he thinks of the alleged attempt to strangle the British navy.

It is satisfactory to notice that a movement is on foot with the object of returning Mr. Claverhouse Graham to Parliament. It will be remembered that, a month or two ago, Mr. Graham gave a very powerful address to the members of friendly and thrift societies in Warrington upon the subject of old-age pensions. If sent to Parliament he will be in a pre-eminent degree a representative of the friendly societies of the country, and particularly of that rapidly increasing proportion of the friendly society world which is favourable to the adoption of a State-aided old-age pension scheme. Mr. Gra- ham is a man of splendid ability and energy and an indefatigable organiser. He seems to have devoted himself to the work of popularising the old-age pension scheme, and the cause could not have a more whole-hearted champion.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, JANUARY 7, IR9B.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

BRUNSWICK WEST EYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN. On Monday evening the annual social" in connec- tion with the above school was held in the school-rooms, Brunswick-street. There was a fair attendance a scholars present. A capital tea was provided by Mr. J. Nancollis, after which the party enjoyed themselves the rest of the evening in games &c., over which Mr. Eden discharged the duties of M.E. Mr. George Johnson gave a song. The party broke up about ten o'clock.

GAS EXPLOSION AT RUNCORN. On Sunday night, about nine o'clock, an explosion was beard at the house occupied by Dr.D.3laney,Church- street, Runcorn. It appears that the doctor noticed a smell of gas aad went with a Mr. Herbert to ascertain the cause, but previously took precaution t) open all the windows in the room. After finding out where the leak- age was ha raised the candle which he had held in his hand in the room and an explosion took plice,and broke four or five panes of glass and damaged the ceiling. No one was hurt. THE NEVI YEAR.

Watchnight services were held at most of the churches and chapels on New Year's EV9. The year 1893 WAR ushered in in the usual fashion by the sounding of buzzers,the whistles of steamers and railway engines,and the ringing of the bells of the Parish Churcq. The first day of the year was not observed as a holidav,and most of the business places were open all day. Monday was observed as a holiday and business was practically sus pended. On Saturday and M .nday the local bands were out playing selections. MARRIED WOMEN'S TEL PARTY AT TELE PRESBYTERIAN

:knout. On Wednesday evening next, at the Presbyterian School, Victoria-road, the married ladies connected with the church are announced to give a tea and an enter. tainment. The other sections of the church, viz., the children, single men, and the married men have given concerts, and now it is the married ladies turn. A god tea will be provided, and special features will he intro- duced in the conceit. It is not often one as a chance of listening to all married artistes, as will be the case on Wednesday at the Victoria-road Presbyterian School.

, CAMDEN SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN. The annual New Year treat to the teachers and elder scholars connected with the above school took place on Menday evening in the school, Lowland-road. There was a fair attendance, presided over by Mr. John Dutton. After tea an entertainment was given, songs. Ac., being contributed by Messrs. W. Littler, J. Hind,S. Bazley, S. Littler, and H. Lloyd. Games, izc., were then indulged in up to a late hour. On Tuesday the children connected with the Camden School had their annual tea. This was served out to them at four o'clock and then games were indulged in up to eight o'clock When they dispersed, oranges were distributed to them

. . A WESTON SHOPKEEPER IN TROUBLE.

Before Sir Richard Brooke and Mr.P.Speakman, at the Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday, Ernest Okell, shop keeper, Weston,was summoned for having exposed in his shop on the 6th December, certain margarine without having attached a label with the word " margarine' printed thereon.—Defendant pleaded guilty.—Thomas Wright, assistant Weights and Measures officer, said he visited the shop of defendant on the 16th December. He asked him if he sold butter, and he replisd he did. Witness asked him the price and he was told it was a id per lb. In serving him the defendant's brother said it was not pure butter, it was mixed. There was no label on it. He did not see any about.—Walter Timmins ear- -1 roborated.—Defendant said he bad the label by the side of the margarine.—The Bench imposed a fine of lOs and costs (£llO3 2d). - -

ANOTHER DROWNING MYSTERY ST RUNCORN. Mr. J. E. Worslev,coronertheld an inquest at the Run- corn Police Station on Thursday, morning, on the body John Ealey which was found floating in the Ship Canal on Tuesday afternoon.—William Thornton deposed to finding the body in the Canal near tho old warehouse. He gave information to the police.—P.C. Carrington stated he received the body from the last witnes. It appeared to have been in water for some time. He re- moved it to the mortuary. There were no marks of vio- lence on the body. In the pocket of the man's clothing he found papers which described the man as "John Ealey,'ship's cook, of the Selina." He entered the ship on the 10th November and was discharged on the 27th. The ship "Selina" was from Penzancs,and when at Run- corn she was lying near the old warehouse where the body was found. Witnes had made enquiries in tho town, at the custom house, and from the agents of the ship but could glean nothing about the man's anteced- ents. The body appeared to have been in the water about a fortnight. The Coroner said the evidence was of a very meagre description and the only verdict the jury could return was one of found drowned. The jury concurred and returned a verdict accordingly.
