CONTERSAZIONE AT THE TECHNICAL INSTITUTE.

The annual conversazione ia connection with the Run- Corn Technical Institute, was held on Monday night, in the Institute. Students were admitted free,and parents and friends were allowed admission on payment of a small charge. There was a large attendance. The rooms and corridors had been daily decorated with flags, ban- nerettes, Chinese lanterns, &e. There was an exhibition of living pictures in the art room which included the arrival of a train in a railway statien,garden scenes anl a number of other beautiful views. These occupied about an hour. A concert was then held under the pre- sidency of Mr. J. Wilson, chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee. The Runcorn orchestral band under the conductorship of Mr. Horace W. Handley gave selections, Mr. Thomas Johnson sang the" Skipper "in a very able manner. He was loudly encored and res- ponded. De also sang "Queen of the Earth." Miss Grounds of Wm ringt,on, and Miss Kirkham of Runcorn sang the duet, " Maying," Miss Kirkham also sang in beautiful style " Twickenham Ferry." For this she was encored. Miss Kirkhatn and Mr. W. Grounds were the accompanists. Another exhibition of the kinematograph then took place in the physical laboratory. Dr. Annett of Runcorn, who is at home for a short holiday from the Berlin University, gave an address on the very highly interesting subject " Bacteriology." The lantern was manipulated by Messrs. C. and H. W. Handley.

RUNCORN PRIMITIVE METHODISTS' SALE_ OE WORE.

On Wednesday afternoon, a sale of work was held in connection with the Runcorn Primitive Chapel. Mrs. Thomason, of Runcorn, opened the days sale of work and said it gave her great pleasure to be present. The object was to provide for an adverse balance which hld arisen from the fact of putting forth special efforts in the early part of last year to renovate and beautify the chapel. There wss also a shrinkage in the ordinary re- venue. She hoped they would be successful in their efforts to realise what they wished for. Mrs. Thomason was cordially thanked on the motion of Mr. J. Richard- son, seconded by Mr. T. Booth. The Rev. W. Fidoe an- nounced that Mrs. Thomason had given a donation of £E towards the funds. RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held on Tuesday night,Mr. James Pritchard presided,and there were present Messrs F. J. Norman, E. Imison C.C., C. A. Timmins, G. Moss (clerk).

'SCHOOL HANAOHMENT CONMITTHE.

The School Management Committee recommended that the resignation of Mr. Crosby, who had obtains 1 an appointment elsewhere, be accepterl, and further that Mr. J. Brown be appointed head teacher at Brunswick boys school, the engagement to be subject to one calen- dar month's notice, it being alse understood that the en. gagement would terminate on the 31st July next, when the school would be closed. After that date Mr. Brown would revert to hie status of certificated assistant tekcher in the service of the Board.—The minutes were approved on the motion of Mr. Norman.

TUE ROLLS.

The Clerk reported that there were on the rolls 3339 children against 3380 in December of the prevlous year a decrease of 41. The average attendance Wes 2783, against 2733, an increase of 30 ; the percentage of attendance was 62.7 against 88.8 It was decided to draw a cheque for £450 for Mr. S. C. Beckett, to complete the purchase of two cottages ad joining Granville-street School, which had been decided upon in 1895. The sanction of the Educatbn Depart- ment was only now to hand.

_ NO FEES TO BE CHARGED IN RUNCORN VOLUNTARY SCHOOLS.

Mr. unison said he was sorry that Canon Wood was not present, because he would probably have announced that if ever fees bad militated against attendance at achool,that had been rernoved,as in future no fees would be charged in the voluntary schools in Runcorn. Every child in Runcorn would be able to attend school with- out payment of any fee. (Hear, hear.)—The Chairman: lam glad to hear it.—Mr. Imison As the Vicar puts it in the "Parish Magazine" the parents get 4s. per head in average attendance. He doubted whether there was another town where there were so many children at- tending schools not under the management of School Boards, where it could be said there was a tree place for every child who wished to go to school. The House Committee reported that they had had the question of the nursing staff at the hospital under consideration, and had decided to recommend the Guar- dians to appoint a coca mittee to deal with it. Mr. Stokes said it was a rather important matter, and they found it would be well to form a representative comtnittee. Mr. Dansey said since the order of the Local Govern- ment Board last August, all pauper nursing had been naade absolutely illegal, and paupers were only allowed to act as attendants after approval by the medical offi- cer.—ln reply to Mr. Dansey, the Master said they had about SO beds.—Mr. Dansey continuing, said they bad got A beds and two nurses. He thought there were very few on that Board who did not think it was absolutely necessary to have a night nurse in the hospital. They knew that many people required a nurse more during the night than in the day. If one of the nurses was worked at intervals during day and night she would not be fresh, therefore, a night nurse was necessary. A committee consisting of eleven members of the Board were nominated as a committee to consider the subject. Mr. Parr: How many patients have you in the hospi- tal.—The Master : 50 chronic.__

SMr. Dansey : There are not 50 confined to bed. The subject then dropped. This concluded the meeting, and the Board rose.

MARKETS AND CEISIETERY COMMITTEE.

Recommended by this committee that Canal Boat No. 1003 be registered ; that the following report prepared by the Secretary as to the execution of the Canal Boats Acts be approved and adopted. "My Lords and Gentle- men,—l am directed by the Urban District Council of Runcorn to present to you the following report as to the execution of the Canal Boats Acts, 1877 and 1884, and the observation of the regulations thereunder tor the year ended 31st December' 1897. During the year five boats have been registered all of which were new boats, making a total of 10,13 boats registered at Runcorn. The Inspector reported 13 contraventions of the Acts and regulations. Particulars as follows :-1 boat over crowded : 6 boats required paitaing ; 1 boat leaky ; 3 boats without certificates on board; 1 boat with number defaced : 1 boat without screens to separate the bads. The• Inspector communicated with the owners with re- gard to the foregoing contraventions, and from time to time reported to the committee Um: the matters com- plained of had been remedied. Legal proceedings were taken against the masters of boat " Daisy " for over crowdirg and of the boat "Oswald " for having a boy over 14 years of age sleeping in the same cabin with his parents. Convictions were obtained in each case. Dar- ing the year two cases of infectious disease were notified as having occurred on canal lonsts. namely a case of diphtheria on the boat "Daisy," and a case of scarlatina , on the boat "Addie." With regard to the boat "Daisy" 'the child died, and the boat was thoroughly disinfected. The boat " Addie " was isolated and when the peeling 'stage of the patient was over the boat was thoroughly disinfected. The disinfection in each case WAS carried ,1 out under the sup •rvisinn of our Sanitary Inspector. ' Alterations have been made in certificates as follows :- 1 Phree certificates with name of owner altered ; one cer- ' tificate with name of owner and boat altered ; no certi• ficates have been cancelled during the year. l'he Inspec- tor has visited 306 boats,4Bs men, 117 women, and 141 children were found on board. The Council note with great regret that there is an increase in the number of worsen and children found on board the boats. For some years there was a decrease. The salary of the Inspector is £2O per annuin,with an allowance of 2s. per boat ex- amined for registration. In last year's report the follow- ing expression of opinion was submitted :—" The Coun- cil respectfully submit that it is their opinion, founded on an experience of twenty years of the working of the Canal Boats Act of 1877, that such Act ought to be amended with a view of prohibing the use of canal boats as dwellings for children." The Council again de. sire me to draw your Lordships attention to the desir- ability of amending the Canal Boats Act of 1877 in the direction indicated. The committee reported that two persons had given incorrect information when giving notioe of interments representing the father and husband respectively as being residents in the Runcorn Urban District, thereby escaping the payment of double fees, whereas they are both non-residents and non-ratepayers. They therefore recommend that the particulars be sub- mitted to the L sw Clerk with a view of taking legal proceedings.—Passed on the motion of Mr. Kirkham, se- cond ed by Mr. Grice. TM! 'WATER SUPPLY.

Mr. Handley said-runa7u-re-h-a-d—l:;en going about the town from usually well iuformed quarters that the Council was supplying water contaminated by sewage, and were consequeatly taking best possible steps to spread typhoid fever and other infections disease broad- cast in Runcorn. It was alleged that the regrettable prevalence of these diseases during the past few months had been owing solely to this cause. In a certificate dated November 18th, 1897, Proressor Boyce, who had made a bacteriological examination of the water supply, stated that there was mo sewage contamination at all,no bacteria that could live in the human frame, and that the water was equal to any ordinary tap water. A circular letter from the Local Government Board with reference to water supplies had been referred to the medical officer, but he ha l not had time yet to make a report to the Health Committee—Mr.lmison painted out that in October and November, 1892, Somerset House made an analysis of the town's water supply, anti described it as free from organic impurity, but drew attention to the large per centage of chlorides present,and added that the suitabi.

lity ot the water for 'dietetic purposes was a question for the Medical Office,' of Health—Mr.lmison inquired if the reports received from the Medical Offiuer on this subject were in existence—Mr. Lightburn depreciated the rais- ing of such a discussion. It was sufficient for the Council and the public to know that the water was pure—The Secretory, replying to Mr. Inoison, said he had no knowledge of the medical officer's report.—Mr. Collier said the analysis had always shown that the water was pure ; the only fault was the excess of chlorides.—Mr. Imison said be wished to know whether the recommendation of Somer- set House was carried out and what was the opinion of the medical officer.—The Chairman said the subject had arisen witholt nntice and they could not put their hands upon the report at once. He hoped that the reports that the water was contaminated with sewage had not sprung from anyone with the slightest interest in the , town.—Mr. Dean said if the Council gave the Health ' Committee an expression of opinion they would adver- tise Professor Boyce's analysis which would effectually silence the rumours that had been floating about.— Mr. Handley proposed and Mr. Speakman seconded that the matter be referred to the Health Committee.—Mr. Collier said they had found these rumours baseless and the water satisfactory.—Mr. Handley's resolution was ultimately _carried.

Mr. Speakman called attention to the polluted condi- tion of the water in the Bridgewater 't.3"..! hoped the Eleaith Committee would take the matter into con- sideration.—The Chairman : They are dealing with it now.

This concluded the businese and the Council rose.

A WARRINGTON TEAM AT LEIGH.

DEFEAT BY 21 POINTS TO NIL.

It was certainly a Warrington team that visited Leigh on Saturday but it would need a great stretch of the imagination to recognise it as being repre- sentative of the Warrington first team. Early in the week it was known that Barber and Bate would be unable to accompany the Warrington fiEteen,but few expected such a wholesale " crying off " of players as took place,and still less did anyone an- ticipate such a crushing defeat as was inflicted by Leigh. When the team gathered at Bank Quay Station shortly after one &Clock on Saturday after- noon it was found that no fewer than seven first team players were absent. Barber and Moores were missing from the three-quarter line,Bate from the half back division,and Barratt, Taylor, Hill and Naylor—the latter player having been suspended by the Northern Rugby Union—from the forward ' ranks. It will therefore be seen that the team which j iurneyed to Leigh was composed at about one-half of the usual Warrington players. Under such circumstances faint hopes were entertained by the few spectators from Warrington,of the visiting team being able to notch a couple of points. The Leigh ground was not in very good condition, and was still hard des- pite the recent rains. Right in front of the grand- stand a nasty patch had been covered with chaff Mr. F. W. Smith, the r4eree appointed, was unable to be preAent and a gentleman from Tylcl.Aley took charge of the whistle. Toe teams lined up as fol- lows :

THE P.S A. MOVENIENE IN WARRINGTON.

REPORT OF THE Y.M.C.A. SOCIETY.

On Sunday afternoon, the firet meeting of another year was held at the Y.M.C.A. Rooms, Friars'-green, Warrington. There was a large attendance of members, and a number of new members were enrolled. Mr. William Clegg, the secretary, was chairman,and in place of the usual chairman's address he read the annual re- port for the last year. Mr. Kent, president, was the speaker, and gave an address oe the motto for the year, " Stand fast in the faith." Mr. Belford and Mr. E. Taylor also took part in the meeting. In presenting the fifth annual report of the P.S.A. Society in connection with the Y.M.C.A., the Secretary said it gave him much pleasure to state that 1897 had been a record year for their movement. The general interest the members had taken in all the meetings had been very encouraging to the officers and committee. They commenced the year with 122 members on the register,and after allowing for deubtful cases,they commenced the new register for the present year with 184 members, a gain of 62 members for the year, They had, however, "93 doubtful cases," which were not accounted for in the foregoing figures. Some bad joined membership more than once during the year. Some through change of eircumtances had left town: others were indisposed. Tvo members had been lost through death, and be was proud to tell them that a number had returned to Sunday schlol work. The total numb r of persons who had attended the meeting during the year was 10,839, or an average of 201 persons at each meeting. They had had four meet- ings in the Parr Hall, at which it was eetimated 5,000 persons attended,which left ahalance of 5,839 for their own rnom,or an average a 117 persons at each meeting, as against an average of 93 ot the previous year, being a gain of 24 at each meeting. The priz3 book system had been fully appreciated during the year. The excel- lent quality of the book matter placed in the homes and families of the members was destined to influence for good the lives of a great many beyond the walls of the institution. During the year they had given 511 books, against 382 of the previous year, an increase of 129 books. The Absentee Committee bad visited a great number of members. Their reports of the kindly wel- come they received gave a stimulus to the committee of this important branch, whilst the results of the visits were very satisfactory. The Thrift and Holiday Club had been in existence through the year, but it had not received the encouragement necessary to report a grand success, whilst those who had availed themselves of this means of saving, fully realised the usefulness of the sys- tem. He trusted that in the present year this section would receive the kindly attention and support of the members. The benevolent fund of the society, though small, bad been the means of helping a number of de- serving cases. Those who had received assistance at their hands tendered their sincere thankfulness for the help they have given. The finances were in a healthy condition. They had paid for prizes alone close upon £5O. The committee who had arranged the musical part of the services deserved their hearty thahke for the admirable choice they had provided, whilst to the ladies and gentlemen whe had kindly come to brighten the meetings they felt greatly indebted. Whilst the choice cf chairman had been left to the discretion of Mr. Hunt, and the speakers to the President, their best thanks were due to them. A more successful series of meetings it wonld be impossible to arrange. Their only regret was that so important a work should be confined to so small a room. Without exception a high spiritual tone had been maintained. The plain, practical truths of the Scripture had been soul - stirring and they had abundant proof that when the Master is correctly portrayed "He will draw all men unto Him." He regretted to say that of the six vice-presidents of the society they had lost two through death ; he referred to the late Dr. Smith and Mr. Thomas Morris,both of whom toook a great interest in the work, and were ever ready to lend a helping hand. The com- mittee had invited Dr. Reginald Smith to take the place of his father,whilst Mr. J. B.lsaac had been asked to fill the vacancy of Mr. Morris.. Both these gentlemen had kindly accepted the invitation.. Mr. J. Charlton Parr had retired from the vice-presidency. The selection Of officers for the present year had been made with the following result :—?resident, Mr. T. Se Kent ; acting eiee.president, Mr. W. Greven ; treasurer, Mr. G. Belford ; secsetary, Mr. W. Clegg ; assistant secretary, Mr. B. S. Stone; orgE.tnist,Mr. Hickman; assistant organ- is jun.; markers, Messre.Lawtem, Red. ditel3 M, rR G. rirts°,lfanrdi and S. Taylor."

RovAL or A I:ocer. Priascann. Mr. F. H. Ashton, who has rendered good service as a local preacher, has, says " The Warrington Circuit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record," " ac- cepted an engagement in the Ipswich Circuit as hired local preacher, and so for -a time at least a familiar figure will be missed from the country pulpits. It is not only there that Mr. Ashton will be missed, but also at Bold-st. and eepeciany from the meetings of the Wesley Guild, towards which he bee aot.ed for the last two years as secretary, while from the beginning he bas felt a deep inter- est in its welfare and taken a leadinz part in ita meetings. The prayers and good wishes of 1,11-8 brethren and of the circuit generally will go wi.th him in his new life." IRISH NATIONAL LRAGUB or GREAT Bnrianr,

WARRINGTON BRANCH. A meeting ef the above branch of the Irish National League was held on Sunday in the Druid's Sankey-strest, Mr. T. C. Dalton president, in the chair. The President read Professor Long's speech delivered in the Lord Mayor's parlour on Thursday last at the meeting called by the Mayor of Manchester to raise funds to alleviate the suffer- ings of the people in the West ot Ireland. It was resolved that the Irish National League hold a special meeting of the Irish people resident in and about Warrington in order that they may take their fair share in the work of helping their starving countrymen at home. It WBR also resolved that a ball be held in Parr Hall, the proceeds to go to the same fund. On the motion of Mr. P. Lane, it was resolved that the best thanks of the meeting be given to Mr. Henry Thornton who, in his goodness of heart so characteristic of him, has given £lOO to the Irish Distress Pend.

LADIES' LETTER.,

VT DEAR NELLIE.

I

Straw hats have given place to felt and hem; land the lovely, but fragile flowers of the summer season are deposed in favour of ostrich and cove's plumes, which, together with osprey, birds, and buckles, are liberally employed on winter millinery. Most of the new felt shapes are lifted at the side, but it is no longer fashionable to wear the hat or toque excessively tilted, as this style has been car- ried to such extremes as to be unbearably common.

Felt hats are so generally worn that some directions for trimming these may be acceptable. The toreador shape is hard and unbecoming in felt. A very fashionable hat has the medium with round brim, generally described as a sailor shape, and the rosettes can be arranged across the back, and gradually narrow off to a point on the left side. The crown is slightly indented at the back, and bows or raised folds of velvet sewn on to the top of the crown have a very stylish effect. All velvet should be cut on the cross, and lightly cross- stitched down each side, and for folds round the crown the velvet should not be less than eight or nine inches, otherwise it will have a poor and narrow effect.

The scarf head-dress is still quite the most modish thing out. It does not suit everyone, and It almost require 3 an 18th century style of hair- dressing ala Romney. But where it is successful it is so in the superlative. Height may he given to the ememble by tying the tulle or chiffon or Ease scarf into upstanding bows, or by the intro- duction of a single ostrich plume or aigrette. If the last-named is bedewed with diamonds, so much the better.

suitable Costume for a young lady takes the form of Place aux dames even though they be small ones, and so first to that useful, and withal pretty frock for a girl of any age from seven to 14, the Russian blouse bodice made in simplest fashion of red serge, with groups of tucks on the shoulders to hold in the fulness which afterwards pouches over a waistband of darker velvet, the velvet being introduced again at the neck and on the sleeves.

Chinchilla is being used very much on evening gowns. One is apt to grow almost rhapsodio over a toilette of satin antique in a shade of doll cerise, made with a flat vest of heart-shaped cerise and silver embroidered ivory satin, the bodice very slightly pouched. The sleeves were to the elbow, finished with a band of chinchilla, and a narrow line of similar embroidery and lace ruffles. The décolletage was bordered with the fur, which also edged the hem of the long trailing skirt.

A fashionable winter costume is in velvet, with narrow chinchilla fur, and a leather waistbelt, with clasps and slides. The lining of the collar and the turned-backed revers is of fine cloth, and there is a delicate embroidery work. The sleeves are distinctly novel with three bell epaulettes, but the pattern can be had with coat sleeves, rather full at the top, if required. Children's evening frocks become each yeat more dainty-looking, the development of skirt dancing probably bringing their attire into greater prominence than formerly; and now the sweetest little frocks for the very young, as well as dresses for girls of larger growth, can be bought most reasonably. Soft washing silks are well adapted for children's wear, made up with tucks and lace Insertions, or accordion pleated (if for dancing), with lace lavishly used on the, generally, yoked bodice. They are very useful to small people, who invariably spill things on their best frocks and mpoil them; the stains wash out when only lace and washing silk is used, and the frock looks as fresh as ever. Nothing can look better for children than these simply-made party frocks, either in all white or colour; soft, delicate shades, not brilliant, crude colours. Your loving JEITY%

A " Cold " is not necessarily the result of a high or low temperature. A person may go directly from a hot ' bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even,and not take cold. He may remain out in the coldest atmos- phere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contary, he may take cold by pcuring a couple of teaspoonfuls of water upon some part of his dress, or by standing in a door or before the stove, or by sitting near a window or other opening. where one part of the body is colder than the other prt. Let it be kept in mind that unif,rmity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon d;fferent parts .4 the body that produces Colds by disturbing uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Beep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food and by a powerful Tonic, and Tau will be proof against a Cold and its results. A. STRONG MAN'S STRENGTH.

(From the Rugby Advertiser.)

Mr. Em TOMALIN, tsf High-street, Long Buckby, a strong man between fifty and sixty, has revealed the se- cret of his strength. It appears that one day just before Christmas, three years ago, he did a bit of work out in the open : it was bitterly ccld,and the wind pierced him to the bone. Rheumatism in all its paintul intensity subsequently set in. His hands, feet, and legs began to swell ; they became extremely painfultand be had to go to bed.

"I could not," Mr. Tomalin told the writer,"lie in one position five minutes together, and was unable to turn without help. My limbs ached, and seemed like fire. My hands were so painful that I dare not lift the bed *lathes with them. The pains and the swelling became worse and worse, and neighbours who came to see me thought my constitution had hopelessly broken up. This went oa for some time,vrhen I remembered having read in a newspaper about Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale- People, and the great public attention that their wonder- ful cures have attracted, so I decided to begin taking them.. lam not going to say the first pill cured me. After a few days, however, I felt I was getting better, and in about five weeks from my first seizure I could do a little work. I had three bests of pills altogether, but had not taken them all before the rheumatics had quite gone. They did rue no much good that I did not find it necessary to diet myself, in fact I had a drop of beer— which they say is net good for rheureatics— whenever I felt I wanted it."

" Then you have great faith in the pills, "I should think so. One of my daughters in service up in London had a swollen knee, camel by carrying . heavy weights upstairs in a big house ; and the London doctor told her she might get batter with rest, but her leg would alvraye be stiff. Well she came home, and took Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and in seven weeks she was better tnan ever she had been in her life. Her leg was cured and the stiffness had completely gone. She took a box of pills back with her and won't be without them now. We always keep them in the house, and twelve months aao last Christmas our youngest daugh- ter had St. Vitus' dance, which she had often been sub- ject to. She took several boxes of the pills, and the complaint seems to have thoroughly left her." As these disorders are of different kinds ,the question may reasonably be asked,"How is it possible for one medicine to cure so many varying complaints ?" The secret lies in the fact that Dr. Willimns' Pink Pills com- bine two very imp3rtant properties. They are a safe and extremely effective tonic, especially tc. the nerv.2B and the spine, suited not only for men, but also and especi- ally for women suffering from the special ailments of their sex ; and tney have the power of enriching and building up the blood. Cmsequently, when they cure, they cure in quite a different way to ordinary medicine, which only combats the signs and symptoms ot disease. Dr.Williams'Pink Pills attack the foundations of disease and eradicater them from the system, not, however, by the weakening process of purging or moving the bowels (as so-called blood purifiers do), but by giving direct strength and nourishment to the blood and nerves. Thus it is that they are able to cure so many different disorders, such as an ma or bloodlessness, rheumatism, sciatica, gout, heart disease, consumption and decline, chronic erysipelas,paralysis, locornotor ataxv,neural gis. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN SI724DAY SCHOOL.

About 120 persons sat down to tee in Bold-street Wes- leyan schoolroom on Menday evening on the occasion of the annual tea and entertainment in connection with the Sunday school. Trays had been kindly given by : Mesdames Collin,Barlow, Potts, Triggs, Davey,J.Carter, Henshall, Holford. Nicholson, Weaver,Giandy, Miss Ben- son, Messrs. W. Silcuele, T. B. Carter, J. B. Isaac,Girime, Guest, Bodle, R. Bailey, and Hartley. On the removal of the cloth, the entertainment was proceeded with. The Rev. William Potts (sueerintendent minister) pre- sided,and he Was supported on the platform by the Rev. I. Gunnell. of Penketh. After singing, and prayer by Mr. S. B. Stringer, a song, entitled " The Gift " was given by Miss Perkins. Mr. JAMES LINGARD, the secretary of the school, then presented the committee's report for the past year. stated that the work of training the young was one of the most important branchee of Christian service, and demanded the earnest, intelligent efforts of those en- gaged in it. The teachers and officers of the school worked together with one common object in view, to train the young and rising generation in the way of truth ; for they know how important for good or evil were the early influesaces of a child's life. l'he number of scholars on the books Wall 309, with an average at- tendance of 106 in the morning and about 200 in the afternoon. During the year 60 of the scholars had atten- ded the required number of times to merit a prize, which would be distributed at the scholars' annual meeting. The number of teachers was 37,a1l being members of the church. (Hear, hear.) They had a library consisting of 410 volames ; this part of their work was under the managemeut of--Mr. Bodle. who would be assisted by Mr. Herbert Parkinson, to whom they gave a very hearty welcome amongst them, and hoped to retain his services for many years to come. During the year the juvenile missionary collectors had raised the splendid sum of £3O 17s. 41., for which they deserved all praise, especially when they, remembered that the greatest part of this sum had been collected in half-pence. To each collector, and to Mr. Jubb, the in- dustrial socretary,they gave their warmest thanks. They had a branch of the Wesley Guild, which tended to pro- mote the spiritual; mental, and social element amongst the young people. The meetings had been very profit- able and enjoyable, and it was to be hoped that all those who had not availed themselves of the special ad- vantages these meetings offered would attend and re- ceive some of the benefits derived therefrom. The com- mittee wished to thank all who had rendered any service whatever in the past—the teachers, superintendents, secretaries visitors, organists, and registrars. They regretted that ill-health had compelled Mr. Hartley to be absent from the school, but it was to be hoped that ore long he would be able to visit again the school in which he had so long and faithfully laboured.They wished every success to Mr. F. H. Ashton, who had been one of their teacher!, but who was about to undertake important duties in another circuit. It was with regret they om tted from the list of officers the names of Messrs. ' Wilson, Perkins. and Mellor. Mr. Wilson has left the town, and they hoped he would be useful to the church In the town in which he had gone to live, Mr. Perkins and Mr. Mellor, they expected, would continue in the school in some new capacity. The most conepicioue change in the staff of the school was ,the retirement of Mr. William Silcock, as the superintendent, after many years of service ; and the appointment of Mr. J.B. Isaac in connection with Mr. Nicholson, to take his place, Both the teachers and the committee wished to recognise the many and varied talents which Mr. Silcock has brought to the performance of his duties, the deep affection he had had for the school, and for the eminent service he had rendered to it in many ways. (Applaus...) They were glad to say that both his interest and service would be retained in connection with the school. Mr. Isaac re- turned to a position he had filled in bygone years with eminent success, and it was hoped that the school under his and Mr. Nichol Son's care would be greatly prosperei. They gave a very hearty welcome to Mr. Lunt, who after an absence of eight years, had so kindly consented to undertake again the o:fiee of school secretary. By united effort they would he able to impreve the condition of the school. If they all did their share of the work punctually, regularly, and earnestly, success would crown their efforts and they would have the :satisfaction of knowing that they had tried to carry out the Saviour's command, "Feed My lamb." (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN remarked that there was on the part of the church and congregation of 13 ald-street a warm affection for the Sunday Scheol,and its work during the ensuing year would be followed by them with very great interest. They all hoped that the year on which I they had now entered would be a very successful year in every department of Christian service, not only at Bold-street, tut in all the societies of the town, and cir- cuit. He thought they might look hopefully forward and pray that the school might he conducted not only pros- perously but in the sense that the highest aim they had in view—the conversion of the children—should be very abundantly blessed. (Applause.) The Rev. J. GUNNELL also delivered an interesting address in which he wished all present a bright and happy New Year. lie proceeded to refer to the pastoral letter which was in the current issue of their " Church Record," and asked for assistance in the conducting of the society classes not only in the town but in all parts of the circuit. Leaders were required in several places and he hoped that the appeal which had been made for volunteers would be responded to. Allusion was also, made to the approaching evangelistic mission of Mr. Nix of Ilondon,and he asked for their prayers and assistance in making the mission a great sutcess. An attractive and most enjoyable musical programme was afterwards given. Miss B Ale was encored for an effective rendering of "Tie Ole Umbrella ;" Mr. Arthur Griffiths received a similar compliment for his singing of " Come into the garden Maud ; and encores were also demanded from Miss Brough and Mr. W. H.Wallington A fine rendering of "The Heavenly Song," was given by Min Perkins. The instrumental items included clarionet solos by Mr. Hipkiss and Miss Winnie Pigot, Miss Bailey recited " Mary,Queen of Scots," and Miss Bodle "The Old Maid," both being given in a manner which greatly pleased the audience. Mrs. F. Pendlebury ful- filled the duties of accompanist.

SAD DEATH OF A 'RUNCORN GIRL AT PANTASAPH CONVENT.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

On Saturday Mr. F. Llewelyn Jones, deputy coroner of Flintshire, held an inquest at Sc. Cl ere' s Roman Catholic Convent, Pantasarh, near flolyweli, respecting the death of a girl named Mary Moran, twelve years of age. . _

-From the evidence given it appeared that the deceased girl and her three sisters were sent to the orphanage belonging to the convent by the Runcorn Board of Guardians about two years ago. When they arrived there they were all in a weak state of health, but had much improved latterly. On Thursday evening the deceased complained of feeling sick, and as put to bed, where she was found dead in the morning. Dr. Williams, of Holywell, who was called in, arrived just after death had taken place. He stated in evidence that the child was very well nourished. He had no knosledge of her previously, and therefore could not give any definite opinion as to the cause of death ; but he considered it to be from natural CAUSCS, probably heart failure. These children frequently Came to the orphanage with constitutions very much impaired. A juryman asked the elector whether he considered it necessary to have an inquest in a case like tnis. Dr. Williams said the death could not be recorded, so it was his duty to report it to the coroner. Besides, he thought it desirable that an irquEst should be held when a person died in an institution of this kind. The Coroner said it was to the public interest to have a full inquiry into a case of this kind, and it was like- wise to tne interest of those rasponsi .le for the conduct of the Institution. The jury at once returned a verdict of death from natural causes, and expressed their Sympathy with the reverend mother.—Dr. Williams, in replying on her behalf, said she felt grieved the New Year should open with death in the house.

"FLAME."

The first of the series of four lectures for children on "Flame," was delivered in the Museum Lecture Room oa Tuesday afternoon by Mr. F. G. Ruddock, FIX. There was only a small attendance. At the outset the Lecturer said he pro- posed to show them experiments in one of the most interesting of phenomena, that of combustion, or burning. From the very earliest ages the earth, sea, sky, air, had attracted the attention of pnilosphers. and a great number of speculations had been started on the composition of these subjects, and the cause II of the great number of changes in them. Aristotle I maintained that all substances were composed of one substance and that this one substance existed in a vast variety of forms. He thought that the four elements were earth, fire, air and water. In the middle ages it was felt that these did not ex- plain anything like all the various things they could see going on and then the philosophers attri- buted all these changes to what were called three elements. One was salt, the other sulphur, and the other mercury,which were supposed to be the three elements of every known form of matter, and vari- ous methods of incorporating these three things produced all the vast substances we now knew. The first thing known about the air was that it had weight, and the lecturer demonstrated this fact by his first experiment. Ho then showed that it was absolutely necessary there should be air before any- thing could burn, and also that air once used in combustion could not be again used. Other simple experiments with oxygen and metals followed, all being explained in a lucid manner,though Mr. Had- dock found difficulty in bringing the language of science down to the understanding of the younger pdrtion of his audience. The lecture occupied an hour, and gave much interesting instruction in the rudimentary properties of "Flame.'

BETTER. KEEP TITE DOOR LOCKED.

There were half-a-dozen of 113—two Englishmen ad four Americans. It was in the month of July of last last year-1893. We had been touring in Scotland for several vi eekg, doing a little shooting aud fishing, and a deal of loafing. On the night I now h Iva in mind, the weather being warm f.r that region, we lay about on the grass near the mountain inn spinning yarns, mostly of alleged personal adventure in vaiious parts of the world.. Oao of the Britons had just finished a thrilling tale of a tiger hunt in India, whsa a sland-r Yankee, the most sedate of the party, slowly moved his pipe from hie mouth and 133 id

" My personal knowledge of tigers is wholy confined to what I have seen of thorn at men tgeries and cir- cuses. but we have in America soma unlicensed beastg sufficiently formidable to inspire a m itary int srest in an ordinary person who happens to meet up with them unexpectedly. About ten years ago my wife and I, with our baby, lived for a thn in Northern New York,on the edge of an extensive forest. It was a lonely I spot, the nearest house a mile away, and I tried to man- age my affairs so as to be at homi every night. One evoning,in summer,we had been sitting and talking tan til it had come to be,perhap3, ten o'clock, when we both heard a light tread in the room directly above. We were on the ground floor, and inasmuch as we supposed our- selves to be the only occupants of the premises, the sound overhead was interesting, to say the leasn, of it. Man or animal, the matter must be looked to, and there was nobody but ma to do it.. Seizing an old axe. handle, the only available vreapin, I lighted my barn lantern and cautiously ascended the stairs. There in the room at the top, with its great yellow eyes shining like these of our friend's tiger in the jungle, was the biggest and ugliest-looking wild cat I had ever seen. As I was starting to retreat he made a leap for me, passing over my shoulder, tearing my, skirt from my back, and with it a goodly strip of my skin and muscla. Then, seeing the outside door open he plunged down the stair- case and so out of the house ; and you will easily believe I was not long in locking the door behind him.

0 How did be get in ?" a ked the tiger hunter.

SOCIAL PROBLEMS.

A ItA.TEPAYERS' QUESTION. By JOSEPH RYDER, General Secretary of the Land Nationalisation Society.

ARTICLE XI.

Among the very few kinds of income which are fit subjects for peculiar taxation, ground rents bold the principal place, being the most gigantic example extant of enormous acces- sions of riches acquired rapidly, and in many cases unex- pectedly, by a few families, from the mere accident of their possessing certain tracts or lend, without their hiving them- sis.ves aided in the acquisition by the smallest exertion, out- lay, or risk.

701 IN STUART MILL (" Political Economy.")

Nothing can be more reasonable than that a fund (ground rents) which owes its existence to the good government of the State should be taxed peculiarly, or should contribute something more than the greater part of other funds towards the support of that government. A/Jilt SMITE( (" Wealth of Natioas.'') I would now invite serious attention to a consideration of one of the principal reasons why Radical reformers on local Councils or Corporations, on School Boards and on Boards of. Guardians, have to encounter such dgeecsidoefd tohpe- spuOpSiptoirt. f t Is there aerpsrowphoesrael tthey a raise etubl de ratherw a receive workmen employed by the Council to a fair " living wage "in order that the public body may become a model employer ? Or is it proposed that the hours of the men should be reduced so as to afford them that amount of leisure which every man has a right to enjoy? Then will arise a loud chorus of dissent and disapproba- tion. Not alone, mark you from those who are commonly interested in keeping the labourers down, but from many whose sympathies are naturally for rather than against them. 'These do not object to the principle of the pro- posals which they help to defeat, so much as to the ex- pense which would be involved thLreby. When any, scheme for the improvement of the town is put forward whether it be for more thorough drainage Cr for street' widening, or for the clearance of slums, or for the adoption of the Libraries Act, it is certain to be vigour- ously assailed, and for a long time set back. Because of the expense. And as with a Town Council, so with a School Board. There are many whose hatred of School Boards, and of what they will lead to, has never yet been overcome ; men who hate popular educ ition for its own sake ; men who think that a working man's child is unatted for "that state of life into which it has pleased God to call him " if he can do much more than write his own name. But educational reformers are met with the powerful opposition of those who are quite ready to admit the right of the poor man's child to the best education which his couutry can give and which be can receive. Their ground of objection is simply the increasing cost entailed by the development of School Boards.

OPPOSITION TO REFORM.

Many men get elected to positions on School Boards for the single purpose of keeping down the education 1 rate. Mr. Diggle, late leader of the so-called ecmotnists on the London Board, once divided the Board five times upon the proposal to purchase a five-guinea sewing machine. And for nine years it was the guiding prin- ciple of that Board to keep the rate down to the smallest amount possible, no matter whether the cause of educa- tion suffered or not. Throughout the country the chil- -1 dren of the poor are handicapped and the cause of educa- tion is crippled by those who are guided by so-called economy. The school classes are too large, the teachers are not numerous enough, the school age is narrowly re- stricted, and school buildings are not kept up to modern requirements. This, of course. is not true economy. It is a penny-wise pound-foolish policy. For there is no more profitable 113 vestment that the money which is spent on national education. If England is to retain her hold on the world's trade, if she is to compete on equal terms with a country like Germany, then she must bring her Who, theirmoreover, outcry educational system up to the level which that country has attained. Yet the Radical reformers who advocate a liberal educational policy are hampered and opposed ttr aevneareyt oturn y by r e theaen e r"a ll economists," loudest in against foreign cempetition,

HIIMANISITIVIN OF THE POOR-Law. Similarly with Boards of Guardians. The humanisa- tion of the Poor-Law system is one of the most urgently needed modern reforms. But the so-called Guardians of the poor are, perhaps in most cases, guardians of the poor-rates first, and guardians of the poor second. An untold misery to thousands is the consequence. Read the reports of those who have gone to the Boards of Guardians full of human sympathy, with a consuming desire to soften the hard lot of those who seek as sup- plicants for the shelter of the " Poor-Law Bastilles." See how the ratepayer is put first and the pauper is put second on ninety-nine Boards out of every hundred. And see how throughout the feeling thus predominates which is expressed in that oft-quoted couplet— Rattle his bones over the s'ones. He's only a pauper whom nobody owns. Do we ask for a national system of old-age pensions, or pensions for the sick and afflicted who cannot support themselves? So that every man and woman may look forward to the future without fear and trembling, knowing that there is a provision made for them to rest from their labours and enjoy the sunset and the evening of life in the liberty of their own homes,and surrounded by their friends ? So that the blind and the crippled may not stand in misery as beggars at the street corners? And so that the widow and the fatherless whose bread- winner has departed may have the friendly band of the Commonwealth held out to thern,takiog,to some extent, the place or the Joe who has gone, and shielding theta from the hard buffotings of an unkind world?

WHERE TO FIND TEE MONEY.

" All very good in theory," says the practical econo- mist," but where is the money coming from to do it with ? " Now, this is a perfectly natural and reasonable question to ask, and Radicals must seriously consider bow it has to e answered. It is not enough to show that such-and-such an improvement is desirable. We must go a star. further, and show that its coat can be met without adding to the heavy burden which the rate- payers at present bear. If we can do this, and it Cer- tainly can be done, then an immense amount of opposi- tion will be overcome, and long-delayed reforms will be hastened. Mr. Macnamara, editor of "The School- master," and tjan educational expert with • national eputation, placed on his programme when fighting at the London School Board election "that the increasing cost of education should be met by tapping a new source ,of public income—viz., land values." And he on both occasions polled the highest number of votes. Re also will popular support be surely forthcoming for any democratic candidates who will be reformers in raising revenue as well as reformers in spending it. As the population increases, and as public intelligence grows, corresponding demands are made upon the public exchequer, and both the Imperial and the local budgets swell in Volume and increase by leap' and bounds. But side by side with the growth in public ex- penditure there arises a public fund:whizh is adequate to Meet it—viz., the rent of the land. That fund is now the private possession of inclividu ale. It is almost en- tirely exempt from taxation. It contributes scarcely anything to public expenditere, and yet without that expenditure it would be non-existent. Under a just and system it would be the chief, if not the sole source of public revenue. Rates and taxes upon industry might then be gradually renuced,and perhaps ultimately extinguished. And the removal of all fiscal burdens from industry would give such an impetus to trade and commerce as they have never yet received.

Ns-yr_ SOURCE CE' REVENUE':

Therefore the settlement of the lanci-4-uestion means, among other things, the employment of land values for public purposes, and as such it is a question which every ratepayer should carefully Study. It means also the finding of a way to tone down and disarm much of the present opposition which Radicals have to encounter, however good their propositions may be. For in huge measure the Tory reaction is a revolt of the ratepayers. many of whom, such as seriall shopkeepers, have a hard struggle for existence already, and dread a forward home policy which would azld to their present burden, whatever the ultimate benefit may be. No one supposes that the whole of the rent of the land should or could immediately be appropriated. The problem is not so simple as this. But two things are immediately prac- tical—viz.,the fair rating of land values, and the appro priation of the unearned increment.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH.

A choir festival was held at Holy Trinity Church on Sunday, and consisted of instrumental and vocal solos, carols, sacred songs, kc., by members of the choir and friends. Mr. Percy Randall presided at the organ, and opened the service with a voluntary, " Songs without words" (Mendelssohn), after which the choir sang " All people that on earth do dwell." Mr. J. Seed followed with a good rendering of " Tho Heavenly Song," and then the choir gave " Silent Night." Master Fred Ran- dall contributed a violin solo, " I know that my Re- deemer liveth." MiB.3 M. Taylor sang " The Better Land," and the choir gave an excellent rendering of the anthem " Behold how good and joyful." Mr. Lewis sang the solo, and Masters Hunt and Mathew the duet. " Dream of Paradise" was sung by Mr. W. H. Harrison, with a violin and 'cello accompaniment by Master Ran- dall and Mr. H. Balmford. A fine interpretation of " Oace in Bethlehem of Judah" was given by the choir, Mr. Booth being responsible for the solo. A short ad dress was giCren by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, CAIRO-STREE U CHAPEL.

The Rev. W. ii. Drummond delivered a New Year's address to young people in Cairo-et. Chapel on Sunday evening. There was a good congregation, and the ser- vice was a very hearty one throughout. The rev.gentle- man took for his text the words "And the light was the life of IMITI"—St JONI i, part of fourth verse. Some ex- cellent advice was given to the congregation, and the selfish life was contrasted with the unselfish and noble life of the Christian. He expressed the hope that they might go forward in the new year with one hope and one purpose, feeling the wonder of the world and the gladness ef religion and the infinite helpfulness of °briar.. And whatever the year might Oring or might take away might God in His:mercy increase and multiply in them and by all means that life which wag the light of men.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIAN.

A meeting of this Board was held in the Board- Room at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning.: Mr. G. Pilling presi4ed, and there were present : Mrs. Clay, Revs. T. R. Matthews, J. k. Riddette, Messrs. J. W. Knowles, R. T. Lowe, W. Thompson, A. J. Jolley, A. Jolley, Dr. Patterson, William Houghton, J.P , T. Halton, J. Ball, S. Davies,William Hickman, T. B. Malkin. J. Warburton, J. Johnson, vith Mr. J. C. Sutton ( clerk), Mr. S. Sutton (deputy clerk),Mr. C. H.Ward (workhouse master), and Mr. A. Wright (superintendent Padgate Schools.) THE CARE OF INFANTS.

Mr. ISIALKIN in referring to the recent order of the Local Government Board with reference to persons re- ceiving infants for hire or reward, contended that it was ungenerous on the part of the Guardians to appoint tilt reeving officers to look after the children without soars little remuneration for their services being given them. If in order he should like to propose that they receive two guineas at the end of the year for the work which had been imposed upon them.— The Clerk said that the relieving officers were brat acquainted with the class of people in ques- tion. It would be out of order to make any appointment at present. The Board might take it for granted that there were no more cases at all so far as they knew in the union. They were simply taking the precaution of the officers being on the alert to find out whether such cases existed. If it was found that they did exist the Board must proceed to the appointment of an in- spector.—Mr. Hickman said the resolution which was passed provided that the relieving officers should receive extrarremuneration if it was found that the Act increased their duties.—The Clerk r If you find that the Act causes more work, you cou'd consider the queotion of - extra remuneration.—Mr. Malkin; You do rind a man something to do if you put him on the alert.—The Clerk suggested that the board should occasionally make a re- vision of the duties of the officers in order to see if any increase in the work had taken place.—The subject then dropped. CHRISTMAS AT THE WORKHOUSE. -

===== 0002601_18980107_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
The MASTER (Mr. Ward) presented a report of how Cl-ristmas was spent at the Workhouse. He stated that the behaviour of the inmates was very good, and every- thing passed cif quietly and satisfactorily. He wished to thank all the friends who contributed gifts, or who graced the proceedings with their presence. THE REV. J. 11. RIDDETTE AND THE INIII CHRISTMAS BEER. Mr. A. J. Jot.city called attention to the action of the magistrates in sending a man to the workhouse for 14 days under an order of detention. The man had been drinking heavily, and while at the Police Station did some damage in the cells. For a good many months the Board bad incurred additional expense purely through cases of this kind. They had three extra assistants con- stantly engaged in the house through such cases, and it seemed to him and to the members of the HOU43 Com- roittee that when they did ;et a chance the people who had caused expense to the ratepayers should be com- pelled to pay something more than the ordinary sum. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Knowles : Something for attendance.—Mr. A. J. Jolley : The charge should be at the rate of 103. per week instead of 53.--The Rev, J. H. Riddette Did the man receive his portion of ale at the Christmas dinner at the Workhouse ? I should like to know whether he did. Here is Mr. Jolley always strong on the point of getting compensation from inmates who come here through al- cohol,and yet at Christmas we always at the ratepayers - expense give beer. It is a very inconsistent position. The man may not have been in at Christmas.—The Chairman : The Master is not sure whether the man had ale or coffee. He fancies it was coffee. (Laughter)—The Rev. J. H. Riddette : I don't want to rob the poor man or the pauper of his beer if it is supplied voluntarily, but I shall certainly oppose the appropriation of putlic money for the pur- pose if lam here another year.—Mr. Halton : Perhaps our worthy friend will make the inmates a present of hiblf a barrel of beer. instead of the cost tailing on the ratepayers. (Laughter)—The Rev. J. H. Riddette ; I leave that in you. You will know the quality better. (Laughter) The subject then dropped. GIFTS TO THE CHILDREN AT PADGATE. The SUPERINTENDENT of Padgate Schools acknow- ledged the gift of a box of oranges,a barrel of apples,and a quantity of bon-bons from Dr. Peacock; one box of Oranges, nuts and sweets from Mr. J. W. Knowles. A CONCERT FOR THE WORKHOUSE INMATES. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY announced that Mr. George Thomp- son ha 3 kindly offered to give anoth.r concert for the pleasure of the inmates. The Board were very much .-idebted to him. (Hear, hear.) The date had not yet been fixed. THE EXTENSIONS AT PADGATE.

A communication was read from the Local Governs ment ..C.knewle(l.-s'i-iii the reteipt of the d.e,ries let:terS With referenee to the alterations an 3 Sclditions to the hospital at Padgate schools, and consenting to the additional expenditure of £BO Bs. in addition to the £l5O previously granted by the Board.—lt was decided to give instructions to Mr. Owen (the architect) for the work to be proceeded with. THE UNION'S CLAIMS ON THE COUNTY COUNCIL. A letter was read from the County Auditor with reference to the claims of the Union on the County Council, and agreeing to pay the following sums; registration £2B 145.; Union:officers, £2,390: contribu- tion towards the cost of pauper lunatics,£l,334 14s. THE TRANSFER OF PARISHES TO THE RUNCORN UNION. The Ci.nalt reported that he attended at the office of the Clerk to the Runcorn Union for the purpose of ad- justing the claims between the two unions in respect of the areas transferred from the Warrington to the Run- , corn Union in 1896. He agreed to the payment of , £lOl 7s. 10d. by the Runcorn Union, and this would determine all financial matters between the unions from that date. He considered it a very satisfactory arrange- ment. Mr. William Houghton : I see that the rates are Increasing in the Rucnorn Union since the separation. The Chairman I know they have done so in my case. THE STRICTURES ON THE ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE. The Rev. J. H. RIDDETTE asked whether any minutes of the Assessment Committee were ever read before the Board. Some strictures had appeared in the local press on that committee, and no opportunity had been given at the Board meetings for the committee to make any statement. He did not wish to offer any comments on these strictures, but simply asked for information.—The Clerk said the only authsritY the Board had was of con- trolling the expenditure of the Assessment Committi s. The committee could not spend anything without the authority of the Board. Beyond that the proceedings of the committee were independent of the Board.—The Rev. J. H. Riddette : We appoint the whole committee ? —The Clerk : The Board does thar,and is limited to the appointment of twelve members. Proceeding, the Clerk - said he followed the matter to which he beleived the Rev. J. H. Riddette referred, and it would be a very ica- portant matter, especially for the townships of the Union if the Assessment Committee could do anything in it. But he could confidently advise them that there was nothing in the matter at all. There was no power which the Assessment Committee could exercise in the direction referred to, and the gentleman who made the statements was mistaken on al- most all the points to which he referred.—The Rev. J. H. Riddette said he had not formed any opinion on the question,but he was struck with the fact that one of their committees had been attacked, and no opportunity had been afforded on that Board tor making any reply. —The Clerk said there was no doubt that the people at Haydock would consider it was an important matter for them if the suggestions made could be carried into effect. The writer stated that the assessment of War- rington was too low by £120,000. If that was so, then the other parishes in the Union were suffering for it, as the levying of the expenses ought to be made on that in- creased assessment. But as the Assessment Committee largely included the Guardians of other parishes it might be taken for granted that they would not submit to such a condition of things if it e-- could be obviated.— The subject then dropped. All the members took part and a very interesting evening closed in the usual way. NEW YEAR'S DAY AT EARLESTOWN. New Year's eve was a bad augury for the birth of the year 1893. As evening closed in rain began to fall and until very late on the atmospheric condi- tions were as depressing as could well be imagined. In spite of this however, a good number of people were stirring, and towards midnight many wended the way towards the various places of worship I where watch-night services wars held. At the stroke of twelve, the sounding of buzzers, and the tring of cannons heralded the birth of the New Year, and congratulations and wishes for a pnv- perous year were abundant. New Year's day was a slight improvement on the previous day, but the weather was not of a promising character. The atmosphere was cold and raw, and in the evening rain again began to fall and continued without in- termission during the night. The only local attrac- tion was a football match between Earlestown and Leyland, in which the home team proved victorious by 3 goals to 1. The usual New Year's parties to the Sunday School children were not held this year, in some cases they having taken place a few days previous, and in others they were po-tpone 1 until a more convenient occasion. All the places of busi- ness in the town were open, but the works were closed, the v &can (shoeing until Wednesday in this 1 Week, OOPONG.—A Judicious Blend of tin Clio:cesi; T11,A.3 23. per lb, Ceylon Tea Pure, Fragrant and Delicious. SAD DEATH OF A BURTONWOOD FARMER.

Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at the Griffin Inn, Peasley Cross, St. Helens, on Tuesday, on the body of James Fairclough, aged 30 years, a farmer of Bearded Barn farm, Burtonwood, who was killed in Fleet Lane, Parr, St. Helens, on New Year's day, through being rim over by the lorry he was driving.—Edwin Fairclough said the deceased was his brother and was 30 years of age. The horse which deceased had out on Saturday was a bit timid at times, but his brother knew it very well as they had had the horse for eight or nine years.— Jeremiah Heyes, farmer, Burtonwood, said that he met deceased with his horse and lorry upon which were vegetables in St. Helens. He went back with him on the way to Burtonwood. They called at several shops on business In Fleet Lane, after calling at one place, deceased came up and shouted to the horse' Go on Doll," and as the horse started he attempted to jump on the shafts, and the wheels passed over him before the horse could be stopped. He was removed to the surgery of Dr. Bites, and afterwards to the Hospital where he died.—The Coroner: They try to jump on the wagon and grasp the reins at the same time, thinking they are smart. We don't want to speak ill of the dead, but this was no one's fault but his own. Was he sober ?—Wit- ness: He was sober enough but had had a drink.— The Coroner : Had he several drinks ? Witness : I could not say. He had two with me.—Mrs. Lucy Ann Gregory. 456 Fleet Lane, who witnessed the accident gave corroborative evidence, adding that she did not think anyone else was to blame but the deceased.—A verdict o'f "Accidental death " was returned.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRI.Cr COUNCIL.

MONTHLY MEETING. An ordinary monthly meeting of this Council was held in the Board Room at the Town Hall, EArlestown, on Monday afternoon, Mr. C. B. F. B won presided, and there were also present : Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, Dr. Patterson, Messrs. P. McMahon, W. Gilmour, F. J. Wood, J. Simpson, J. Slee, E. r. Egans, T. Ormand. with Mr. W. W. TRH GAS MANAGER'S REPOR Mr. Swann in his monthly report submitted a state- ment with reference to street lighting with incandescent and ordinary burners. The Council had now 93 lamps fitted with incadescent burners, or 101 incandescent burs.ers. The estimated cost for the last three months had been £7O 2s. 101 , as compared with the estimated cost of £7O 10s. 10s. of a like number of ordinary bur- ners. The very severe storms which had taken place during the above mentioned time had been injurious to the incandescent lights. During the month repairs had been made to a defective gas service in Earle-st. near the lifting shop, and a defect in the gas main in the same street near the spring shop ; also to a broken main pipe at the top and middle of Emmett's Brow. During the month they had reputed defective mains in Vita-road and Emmett's Brow. AU hydrants had been examined and tested, and were found and left in good working order. Consumption of water as per register- ing of meters :—Newton Bridge meter, 3,190,000 gallons Viaduct Works meter, 2,080,000 gallons ; Parkside meter, 1,000 gallons ; total 5.271,000 gallons : corres- ponding month last year, 5,102,000 gallons ; increase 163,000 gallons.—The Chairman of the Gas and Water Committee at a meeting on the 20th of December, read a report stating, that as requested at the last meeting of the committee, he had In conjunction with the Clerk and the Gas Manager, enquired late the present system of inspecting gas meters, and recommended that a com- petent meter inspector should be appointed to hive charge of the whole of the meters, and that he should work from the Collector's Office at the Town llall. After a discussion, it was decided to continue the present system for ansther quarter. Letters, dated November 29th, 1697, and December 13th, 1897 were read from Messrs. McCorepodale and Co., Limited, drawing at- tention to the difficulty they experience from time to time in obtaining a full supply of gas sufficient to meet their requirements ; also requesting an additional supply of gas for at least no more lights and large gas engine. It was resolved that the letters be referred to the Gas Works Extensions Committee. A letter, dated December 14th. 1897 was also read from the Vulcan Foundry, Limited, requesting the Council to consider the ques- tion of supplying them with gas for gas engines, It was tee ,Iced that the letter be referred to the Gus Works Extensions Committee. NEWTON CONGREATIONA.L SUNDAY SCHOOL.

RE-OPENING SEIRVICE3.

This school, which has b.ters undergoing restoration for the past six months, has now been reopened, and in connection with tie event special services were held in the Cnapel on Sunday,when the Rsv. J. Hirst Hollowell occupied the pulpit and preached inetrucsive sermons. In ths afternoon a cantata " Ilia day of the bird," was given by the choir, under the direction of Mr. Knight, organist. The soloists/were Mrs. Terrier, Miss Mather. Mr. Hitchen, and Mr. Turner. Collections were made at each service tewards the cost of the school altera- tions, which will amount to about £1,200. Toe work has been excelleetly done,and reflects great credit upon the contract,r, Mr.J. B. Wright, of Leigh. The defects in the structure were first of all discovered in the roof, which has been completely taken off, and the in. tenor of the building cleaned down, and washed , to prevent dry rot. Toe floors 'have been taken ' up for about a yard deep, the new flooring which bas been put down being of pitch pine. The same process has been used also in connection with the ceiling, which is 4 polished pitch pine boards fixed diagonal,y, with suitable ventilation and brackets and supports to cor- respond, resting on stone corners. The room is illumi- nated by means of foot handsome gas burners,eack con- taining 14 jets. The old gallery has been taken down, and the stained glass window facing Crow-lane refilled, and it now look. exceedingly cea4te. The large room as it now stands is Slit. 6ia. by 40ft., and 253. high from the flooi to roof. At the back a new tea room 141 t. 6.n. by Bft. 6ia. has been erected with oth.sr outbuildinge ad sanitary conveniences upon the most improved system. The new porches forming .the front entrance are firted with swing doors of pitch pine. The walls are of Keen's cement and the swing doors in the lobbies ef ernhosse glass. The work was begun at the latter end of July, and the architect is Mr. Axon, of Leigh. On Monday eveoiag the re-opening was celebrated by a social evening and concert. There were about 200 present to tea,which was arranged by the lady members of the congregation.

The Rev. Jsmes Allatt preside3,and la his opening re- marks said ha was glad to see such a brilliant gathering, as he had no idea they would turn up in such large numbers. Some years ago he thought of resigning, but owing to the very kind letters he received from friends whose advice he was in the habit of taking and from the kind expressions made,he was influenced to stay another two years. Instead of that, he was with them now to see this grand work completed. In 1878 there was a similar debt on the chapel, but they had to face it with courage and in a very few years it was wiped of and they would have to face this expense with the same courage, and what they were able to do in those days, the present congregation would do. That, however, was not the school, it was only the school room in which they were now met. The school Wal the boys and girls who met together Sunday after Sunday. He hopeo they should find many who were able and willing co enter into the work of building up the. school within the schoolroom. ' (Applause.) EXIIGRATION TO CANADA.

A consilerable emigration takes Place every year from the United Kingdom, son:e of which goes to Canada, some to the other Colonies, and the larger pro- portion apparently to foreign countries.Canadtans would like to see a much greater part of this movement going 'to Canada, which offers advantage to immigrants not excelled by any ether part of the world. The various provinces. Q iebea, Oatario, Manitoba, and North-west Territories, and British Columbia stretch from the At- lantic to the Pacific Ocean, and offer a wide vane y of climate to suit all canters, and a fertile soil which has been highly spoken of bytho tenant farmer delegations which have visited the country in recent years- a fringe of the territory is at present inhabited,and there are countless; millions of acres of fertile soil ready. with cultivation, to grow all the products of the temperate z one, and to provide happ v homes for large numbers of peoele. All agricultural produce grown in the United Kingdom and in Europe is raised In Canada as well as many other articles that are nnt cultivated to any extent in Great Britain. The export of farm and dairy produce from Canada is considerable. In all the provinces unlimita 1 opportunities exist for the investment of capital, either in manufactures or in the natural industries connected with the forests, the mines,the fisheriee,and agritsulture. Toe mineral development both cf Ontario, of British Columbia, and of the Yukon district,is attracting much a•temion. Psrsons with moderate incomes 4111 find ICI 01' Domini,sn many advantages not to be obtained else- where, and for those with growing families,the facilities for education and for starting young men and young women in life deserve special consideration. Tne classes especially desired are those with capital. persons with moderate incomes, and farmers, farm labourers, and domestia servants. Free grants of HO acres of land are i given to male settlers, above the age of 18 years, n toisnitoba and the N orth-west Territories. Crow n lands can be obtained in the ()the. provinces either free or on nominal terms,while improved farms, ready for occupa- tion and cultivation, ins 9 be purchased in every part of the D siniaims at reasonable priests. Fill information may be obtained from the Canadian Government Agency, 15, Water-street, Liverpool.

Transfer regulations,as at pre3ent existing, will not be interfered with in the case of paid players as between one competition club and another. In the case of non- comrtition clubs,at present players may apply for a I transfer, and if refused the refusing 01ub3 must prove that illegal inducements havo been offered,failing which I proof the transfer is granted. What course will be fol- lowed in the event of a non-competition player wishing to become a professional with a competition club—and vice versa—is a matter which has yet to be decided. It has teea understood that a twelve months' residen- tial qualification will be enforced with regard to players from outside counties and countries. That propoial is in the nature of an alternative to the legislation of pro- fessionalism. If professionalism be Ie lisd , such a qualification will not be proposed. A. copy if the proposals of the Sub-Committee has to be sent to each member of the Northern Union Com- mittee, with the view of full consideration by the vari- ous club committees. The Committee of the Union will meet at Huddersfield on the 18th inst. specially to hear the views of clubs and come to a decision as to the scheme to be adopted. If necessary, a special general meeting of all the clubs in the Union will be held to re- vise the professional by-la q;, failing which the necessary legal alterVions will be made at the annual general meeting in July. No alteration will take place during to current season.

After devotional exercises,the Rev. John Yonge ascended the pulpit and delivered an address on " Congregational principles." Ile said such a subject had usually been regarded as a proper and even needful thing on such an occasion as the present. No one could truthfully accuse them of too frequently vitera thug or obtruding their distinctive ecclesiastic views. There were those who said their churches were not cite rches ; that they were nothing better than schismatic sectarians ; that they were people eeith crotchets rather than convictions. They were people who must be tolerated, but could hardly be respected. Occasions like the present, there- fore, might fitly be utilized for setting forth the scrip- tural and conscientio us reasons for a Congregational polity. Very certain was it that their Nonconformist forefathers knew thoroughly well the reasons for their ecclesiastical preferences. They were true and staunch in their adhesion to the congregational idea, because fiemly persuaded that it was the idea warranted by the teaching of Christ and His apestlee, They rejected the episcopal idea of the Church and the State establishment of religion, beeves° they held those ideas to be entirely unwarrante dby the New Testament. What they Con- gregationalists should apprehend was the simple fact that the lapse of time had not made that to be an error which was once a truth, and that if their nonconformist fore- fathers were right in their nonconformi tv, and right in their congregational principlee,it was not riget for them to-day to drift away from these pesitions, or to sacrifice these principles. There was.unhappily, a very frequent drift in this direction,and it was therefore becom jug ex- tremely needful to check and counteract it by careful exposition of what they held to be spiritual and eccles- iastical truth. There were some Chi istian people who thought that Ch urch principles and forms of worship were of no real importance. They thought it mattered very little where or how they worshipped God, or under what denominational banner they enrolled. It was no doubt perfectly true that salvation was obtainable in any church, or,for that matter,outside any church. The Saviour was not the church,but Christ. The New Testa- ment did not stipulate any ecclesiasticism into which men might go if they were to have spiritual life, but it presented and proclaimed a Divine Per- sonal Saviour who would have all rnea ce me unto Him that they might have life. "The Church, which is His Body "comprised many sections, bearing many different names, but all its members were one in the faith, hope, peace and life that were in and by Christ Jesus. I t did not follow however, that because of this church principle was of no consequence. A man could live in any house or society or state which fur nished him with means of existence. It would be absurd to say that therefore it mattered nothing where he liyed how he lived, or with whom he lived. They heard much about the importance of the influence of environments. They knew that physical and moral effects were thus produced. They might therefore, be confident that great spiritual effects were produced by ecclesiastical environments and spiritual atmospheres. Surely an intelligent man would seek and choose that spiritual dwelling, atmosphere and environment which could best conduce to his spiritual 'development, growth and freedom. la spiritual respects ' the church was meant to ba to a man what home, kin- dred, friendships were ira temporal respects. It should makehim realize the height and breadth and individual- ity of his nature. It should make him feel in every sense a man—having independence of judgment and freedom of action. The ideal church was that which had In it at least the suggestion and forecast of the ideal human society. Its watchwords were those which had been miedeemed revolutionary and destructive, but which were really redernptive,reconstructive—" liberty, equality, fraternity." Now, he ventured to affirm that no ecclesiastic system answered 5) completely. to such an ideal as did Congregationalism. He esplained a Congregational church to be a community of Chris- tian people combining for the worship of God, the promotion of their spiritual life, and the extension of the kingdom of Clerist. This society was capable ' of managing its own affairs. Every person in it was I of equal rank and authority with every other. I The late greatly revered Congregational historian, Dr. John Stoughton, in an essay on the Primitive Churches had said, "the earliest iciea of a church is that of a brotherhood for the maintainence and diffusion of religious convictions." This principal was at the root of Congregationalism. They held that church member- ship could not be determined by any accident of natural birth, or of human citizenship, or merely intellectual acceptance of a creed or a ceremony. The difference between them and those who believed in a National Church was the theory that the nation, as such, was the church,and that the church was the nation. They did not believe that any such politically created insti- tutions could be in any scriptural sense a church. Three things were essential in congregatioualism, viz.— " A church is a spiritual brotherhood consisting of personal believers in the Lord Jesus Christ ; a church as a complete society capable of managing its own affairs, in loyal obedience to the will of Christ, and to brotherly love; the church is independent of all externalautherity, answerable to Christ alone." The Rev. Dr. Fairbairn delivered the charge to the pastor.
