THE NORTHERN UNION.

THE NEN PROFE3BIONAL PROPOSAL 3.

IThe new proposals re profe6sionalsm brought forward by th. Sob-C manttee of the Northern Union have,says Yorkshire Post," neurally created great in- terest in Rugby football circles. Fuller details of these proposals will, therefore, be acceptaole to all concerned in thu settlement of the most important question which has yet been raised in connection with the nandling code. As announced in the Press on Wednesday, the Sub- Committee of the Northern Union brought forward a report which was laid before the full Committee of the Northern Union at Huddersfield on Tuesday evening,and at the same meeting the report of a minority was pre- sented. Concerning the latter, it may be said that it emanated practically from One individual, and in effect was a well-meant effort to insist upon the carrying out to the letter and the spirit the present regulation re- garding the payment for "broken time." The majority of the members of the Sub-Committee, however, felt compelled to acknowledge what has long been patent to the public—that the " broken time " regulations are being broken all round, and that a recognition of full prefessionalism is now inevitable—in fact, is absolutely neCe+Sary in toe inrereits of the Union itself. It is tlaerefsre proposed, as the first clause, that the psyment of players be legalised. No restriction on the emount of payment is made,for the simple reason that it would be impossible to enforce it if adopted. The key- note of the Sub-',Tommittee's policy and proposals is that while professionalism must be recognised and sanctioned it must be made imp nsible for a man to earn his living solely by playing football. To carry out this principle, it is proposed that every player must be engaged in a bona-fide situation. A register of the employment of all professional players is to be kept by the clubs, and fur- nished to the Secretary of the Northern Union, and in csses where men are out of employment they will not be permitted to play football. A breach of this regulation will render a club liable, for Me first offence., t a severe monetary fins—the amount of which has yet to be fixed —and for is subsequent offence to such term of suspen- sion as the Committee may decide. In all eases the deci- sion of an offence will rest, not with the Competition Comenittees,but with the full Committee of the Northern Union. The ones of proving that a man is properly employed will rest with the club,and superfioial engage- ments, such as those of public-house waiters, grounds- men, and so on will not be recognised as bona-fide situa- tioas. Trie main object the Sub-Committee have in view is to insist that no player must be dependent upon foot- ball for his livlihood—that he must have a legitimate occupation in life outside football—thus striking at the root of the great objection to football professionalism, namely, that when a Waver's day is over he is left with- out means of subsistence, and with only bitter memory of wasted years to support him. Tha',wa repeat, is the key-note of the Sub-Coreinittees's policy. If found possible tc carry it into effect, it will he found a most important departure in the methods of professional foot- ball, and will disarm the greatest objection to profes- sionalism which the most sincere sportsmen entertain. Whether the scheme is a workable one remains les be seen. Bnt at any rate the Sub-Committee of the North- ern Union being brought face to face with inevitable professionalism, are to be cemmendcd on having made a sincere attempt to grapple with the most difficult prob• lone that sportsmen and men of the world could be asked to solve. . . .

Another important point dealt with in the sub-com- mittee's report has reference to the status of amateurs. It is felt that it is necessary to recognise the position of amateurs by placing them under different regulations to these of the paid player. At the same time a loop-hole must not be afforded for pseudo-amateur amateurs to play fast and loose with clubs and with amateur prin- ciples. It is, therefore, proposed that every amateur player shall be free to elect at the commencement of each season what club he will play with,and to do so without a transfer from the club of which he was the previous year a member. Having made this selection,and notified it, he will not be permitted to play with any other club during the sasan.

SAIIIRDA. NIGHT POPULAR CONCERTS.

The series of popular concerts promoted by the Warrington Corporation were never better repre- sented than by the one given in Parr Hall on the evening of New Year's Day. In contrast also with that on Christmas Eve it was very well attended, vacant seats being scarce in all parts of the ball. The company of artistes was unusually strong,each and all of the performers giving satisfaction theagh two, at least, of them have made several appearances before Warrington audiences this sea- son. The Chester Cathedral Quartette were new to the majority of those present on Saturday evening and this fact, added tlio their harmonious singing, won for them a flattering reception. They are no mean followers in the steps of the famous Meitter Glee Singers, and are the strongest and best ba- lanced quartette we have beard recently. With tune- ful voices in almost perfect accord, their rendering, of " Here's life and health to England's Qieen,' "Simple Simon," and " Annie Laurie," was a rare treat. The quartette were encored on each of four occasions, and responded with such catchy songs as" There was a boy," which did not fail to please. Miss Hubi-Newcomhe was again very successful. Her rendition of Rebecca's recitative and prayer from " Ivanhoe," was a fine performance, and merited the warm plaudits the talented young sinzer received. Cowen's pretty song " The Swallows " was vivaciously interpreted by Miss Newcombe in the second part, and being once more recalled she gave that charming little air from the " Geisha " " The Goldfish." The othsr lady vocal- ist,Miss ;eda Phillips, is the possessor of a powerful and tuneful contralto which was heard to advan- tage in " Guiding Light" and ",The Dear Home- land." She sang with taste, aria for a recall gave "The Children's Home." At the risk of " wearing ' out her welcome," as the saying is,Miss Kate Bees- , ley appeared onco more,and the fact that she failed to get an encore slightly indicates that there can be too much of a good thing even at a popular con- cert. Her two recitations, " The Story of a Faithful Soul," and " A New Year's Party " were distinctly good,but the artiste's partiality for gesture was rather too noticeable to be the enhancement of the recital that it should be. Mr. Nelson Jackson is ra- pidly increasing his popularity in Warrington. He is undoubtedly one of the most accomplished hu- morists we see here, and though far from well on Saturday evening, he contrived to be funny enough to elicit roars of laughter from the audience. He is strong on anec- dotes and possesses a repertoire of choice stories that would be hard to beat. They are not "chestnuts," and the inimitable way in which he tells them is a striking feature. His best sketch was ''.Jellico's Play," an original melodrama of a sanguinary type, with a weird and wonderful plot, which completely convulsed his bearers. An ex- cellent tenor vocalist, Mr. A. F. Thornborough, one of the Chester Cathedral Quartette, was unfortun- ately rather "off form," and though he sang with fine expression " I am Waiting," and " Lova's Re- quest," his voice was plainly not at its best, and the songs met with but an ordinary reception. The accompanist, Miss Sophie Roberts, L.R.A.M. again displayed all that skill of adaptation to her task which has made her a favourite. Iler piano solo, "Polka de la Heine," was played with ability, and a finish of execution which was a high tribute to her powers.
